# Racism in the United States

|  | This article **may be [too long](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Article_size) to read and navigate comfortably**. When this tag was added, its [readable prose size](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:SIZERULE) was 17,000 words. Consider [splitting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Splitting) content into sub-articles, [condensing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Summary_style) it, or adding [subheadings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Section#Subsections). Please discuss this issue on the article's [talk page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:Racism_in_the_United_States). *(August 2024)* |
| --- | --- |

&#x20; [Racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism) has been reflected in discriminatory laws, practices, and actions (including violence) against [racial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_\(human_categorization\)) or [ethnic groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups) throughout the [history of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_United_States). Since the early [colonial era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_history_of_the_United_States), [White Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Americans) have generally enjoyed legally or socially-sanctioned privileges and rights that have been denied to members of various ethnic or minority groups. [European Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Americans) have enjoyed advantages in matters of citizenship, criminal procedure, education, immigration, land acquisition, and voting rights.

Before 1865, most [African Americans](2154.md) were [enslaved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States); since the abolition of slavery, they have faced severe restrictions on their political, social, and economic freedoms. [Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States) have suffered [genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_genocide_in_the_United_States), [forced removals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_removal), and [massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Indian_massacres_in_North_America), and they continue to face [discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contemporary_Native_American_issues_in_the_United_States#Societal_discrimination_and_racism). [Hispanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans), [Middle Easterns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Eastern_Americans), [Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans), and [Pacific Islanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Islander_Americans) have also been the victims of discrimination.

Racism has manifested itself in a variety of ways, including [ethnic conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_racial_violence_in_the_United_States), [genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_genocide_in_the_United_States), [slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States), [lynchings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States), [segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation_in_the_United_States), [Native American reservations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_reservations), [boarding schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_boarding_schools), racist [immigration](4599312.md) and [naturalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalization) laws, and [internment camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internment).[^cite_note-1]

Formal racial discrimination was largely banned by the mid-20th century, becoming perceived as socially and morally unacceptable over time. Racial politics remains a major phenomenon in the U.S., and racism continues to be [reflected in socioeconomic inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_inequality_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-2][^cite_note-4] Into the 21st century, research has uncovered extensive evidence of racial discrimination, in various sectors of modern U.S. society, including the [criminal justice](#Criminal_justice_system) system, [business](#Business), the [economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_discrimination), [housing](#Housing_and_land), [health care](#Health), the [media](#Media), and [politics](#Politics). In the view of the [United Nations](31769.md) and the [U.S. Human Rights Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Human_Rights_Network?action=edit\&redlink=1), "[discrimination in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discrimination_in_the_United_States) permeates all aspects of life and extends to all [communities of color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_of_color)."[^cite_note-5]

## Aspects of American life

Main articles: [Culture of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_United_States), [Culture of the Southern United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_Southern_United_States), and [Society of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_of_the_United_States)

### Citizenship and immigration

The [Naturalization Act of 1790](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalization_Act_of_1790) set the first uniform rules for the granting of [United States citizenship](25151566.md) by [naturalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalization), which limited naturalization to "free white person\[s]," thus, excluding [Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States), [indentured servants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indentured_servitude), [slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_slavery), [free Blacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Negro) and later, [Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans) from citizenship. Citizenship status determined one's eligibility for many legal and political rights, including [suffrage rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_rights_in_the_United_States) at both the federal and state level, the right to hold certain government offices, the right to serve [jury duty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jury_duty), and the right to serve in the [United States Armed Forces](32212.md). The second [Militia Act of 1792](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Militia_Act_of_1792) also provided for the [conscription](5735.md) of every "free able-bodied white male citizen".[^cite_note-6] Tennessee's 1834 Constitution included a provision: "the free white men of this State have a right to Keep and bear arms for their common defense."[^cite_note-7]

The [Treaty of Dancing Rabbit Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Dancing_Rabbit_Creek), made under the [Indian Removal Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Removal_Act) of 1830, allowed [Choctaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choctaw) Indians who chose to remain in [Mississippi](16949861.md) to gain recognition as U.S. citizens. They were the first non-European ethnic group to become entitled to U.S. citizenship.

The [Naturalization Act of 1870](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalization_Act_of_1870) extended naturalization to Black persons, but not to other non-white persons. The law relied on coded language to exclude "aliens ineligible for citizenship," which primarily applied to immigrants of Asian descent.[^cite_note-8]

Native Americans were granted citizenship in a piecemeal manner until the [Indian Citizenship Act of 1924](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Citizenship_Act_of_1924), which unilaterally bestowed blanket citizenship status on them, whether they belonged to a [federally recognized Tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_federally_recognized_tribes_by_state) or not. However, by that date, two-thirds of Native Americans had already become U.S. citizens through various means. The Act was not retroactive, so, citizenship was not extended to Native Americans who were born before the effective date of the 1924 Act, nor was it extended to Indigenous persons who were born outside the United States.

Further changes to racial eligibility for citizenship by naturalization were made after 1940, when eligibility was extended to "descendants of races indigenous to the [Western Hemisphere](73162.md)," "Filipino persons or persons of Filipino descent," "Chinese persons or persons of Chinese descent," and "persons of races indigenous to India."[^cite_note-9] The [Immigration and Nationality Act of 1952](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_and_Nationality_Act_of_1952) now prohibits racial and gender discrimination in naturalization.[^cite_note-10]

During the period when only "white" people could be naturalized, many court decisions were required to define which ethnic groups were included in this term. These are known as the "[racial prerequisite cases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Definitions_of_whiteness_in_the_United_States#Racial_prerequisite_cases)," and they also informed subsequent legislation.[^cite_note-11]

### Voting

The [Fifteenth Amendment to the United States Constitution](31667.md) (ratified in 1870) explicitly prohibited denying the right to vote based on race, but delegated to Congress the responsibility for enforcement.

During the [Reconstruction era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstruction_era), African Americans began to run for office and vote, but the [Compromise of 1877](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compromise_of_1877) ended the era of strong federal enforcement of equal rights in the Southern states. White Southerners were prevented, by the Fifteenth Amendment, from explicitly denying the vote to Blacks by law, but they found other ways to [disenfranchise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disenfranchisement_after_the_Reconstruction_era). [Jim Crow laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow_laws) that targeted African Americans, without mentioning race, included [poll taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poll_tax_\(United_States\)), [literacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy_tests) and comprehension tests for voters, residency and record-keeping requirements, and [grandfather clauses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grandfather_clause) allowing White people to vote. [Black Codes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Codes_\(United_States\)) criminalized minor offenses like unemployment (styled "vagrancy"), providing a pretext to deny voting rights. Extralegal violence was also used to terrorize and sometimes kill African Americans who attempted to register or to vote, often in the form of [lynching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States) and [cross burning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross_burning). These efforts to enforce white supremacy were very successful. For example, after 1890, less than 9,000 of Mississippi's 147,000 eligible African American voters were registered to vote, or about 6%. Louisiana went from 130,000 registered African American voters in 1896 to 1,342 in 1904 (about a 99% decrease).[^cite_note-12]

Even Native Americans who gained citizenship under the 1924 Act were not guaranteed [voting rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_voting_rights) until 1948. According to a survey by the [Department of Interior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_the_Interior), seven states still refused to grant Indians voting rights in 1938. Discrepancies between federal and state control provided loopholes in the Act's enforcement. States justified discrimination based on state statutes and constitutions. Three main arguments for Indian voting exclusion were Indian exemption from real estate taxes, maintenance of tribal affiliation and the notion that Indians were under guardianship, or lived on lands controlled by federal trusteeship.[^cite_note-Peterson-13]<sup>: 121 </sup> By 1947, all states with large Indian populations, except [Arizona](21883824.md) and [New Mexico](21649.md), had extended voting rights to Native Americans who qualified under the 1924 Act. Finally, in 1948, a judicial decision forced the remaining states to withdraw their prohibition on Indian voting.[^cite_note-Bruyneel-14]

The [civil rights movement](49001.md) resulted in strong Congressional enforcement of the right to vote regardless of race, starting with the [Voting Rights Act of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act_of_1965). Though this greatly enhanced the ability of racial minorities to vote and run for office in all areas of the country, concerns over racially discriminatory voting laws and administration persist. [Gerrymandering](42223515.md) and [voter suppression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_suppression_in_the_United_States) efforts around the country, though mainly motivated by political considerations, often effectively disproportionately affect African Americans and other minorities. These include targeted [voter ID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_identification_laws) requirements, registration hurdles, restricting vote-by-mail, and making voting facilities physically inconvenient to access due to long distances, long lines, or short hours. The 2013 U.S. Supreme court decision *[Shelby County v. Holder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shelby_County_v._Holder)* struck down the pre-clearance provisions of the 1965 Act, making anti-discrimination enforcement more difficult.

In 2016, one in 13 African Americans of voting age were disenfranchised, which was more than four times greater than that of non-African Americans. Over 7.4% of adult African Americans were disenfranchised compared to 1.8% of non-African Americans. [Felony disenfranchisement in Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felony_disenfranchisement_in_Florida) disqualifies over 10% of its citizens for life and over 23% of its African American citizens.[^cite_note-15]

### Criminal justice system

Main articles: [Race and crime in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_and_crime_in_the_United_States) and [Race in the United States criminal justice system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_in_the_United_States_criminal_justice_system)

See also: [Criminal justice reform in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal_justice_reform_in_the_United_States), [Human rights in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_the_United_States), [Incarceration in the United States](1021698.md), [Police brutality in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_brutality_in_the_United_States), [Race and the war on drugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_and_the_war_on_drugs), [Racial bias in criminal news in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_bias_in_criminal_news_in_the_United_States), and [Race and capital punishment in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_and_capital_punishment_in_the_United_States)

Racial disparities in the share of prisoners, police officers, people shot by police, and judges in the United States in the late 2010s

There are unique experiences and disparities in the United States, in regard to the policing and prosecuting of various races and ethnicities. There have been different outcomes for different racial groups in convicting and sentencing felons in the [United States criminal justice system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_criminal_justice_system).[^cite_note-United_States_2008-16][^cite_note-17] Experts and analysts have debated the relative importance of different factors that have led to these disparities.[^cite_note-Stephan_Thernstrom_1999_273-18][^cite_note-Bobo,_Lawrence_D._2006-19]

Academic research indicates that the over-representation of some racial minorities in the criminal justice system can, in part, be explained by socioeconomic factors, such as poverty, exposure to poor neighborhoods, poor access to public education, poor access to early childhood education, and exposure to harmful chemicals (such as [lead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lead%E2%80%93crime_hypothesis)) and pollution.[^cite_note-\:5-20][^cite_note-\:6-21][^cite_note-22][^cite_note-23][^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25][^cite_note-26][^cite_note-27][^cite_note-28] Racial [housing segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_segregation_in_the_United_States) has also been linked to racial disparities in crime rates, as Blacks have, historically, and to the present, been prevented from moving into prosperous low-crime areas, through actions of the government (such as [redlining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redlining)) and private actors.[^cite_note-29][^cite_note-30][^cite_note-31] Various explanations, within [criminology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminology), have been proposed for racial disparities in crime rates, including [conflict theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conflict_theory), [strain theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strain_theory_\(sociology\)), [general strain theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_strain_theory), social disorganization theory, macrostructural opportunity theory, [social control theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_control_theory), and [subcultural theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subcultural_theory).[^cite_note-32][^cite_note-33][^cite_note-34]

Research also indicates that there is extensive racial and ethnic discrimination by police and the judicial system.[^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36][^cite_note-37][^cite_note-auto3-38][^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40] A substantial academic literature has compared police searches (showing that contraband is found, at higher rates, in whites who are stopped), bail decisions (showing that whites with the same bail decision as Blacks commit more pre-trial violations), and sentencing (showing that Blacks are more harshly sentenced by juries and judges than whites, when the underlying facts and circumstances of the cases are similar), providing valid causal inferences of racial discrimination.[^cite_note-\:19-41][^cite_note-\:25-42][^cite_note-\:23-43][^cite_note-\:33-44] Studies have documented patterns of racial discrimination, as well as patterns of police brutality and disregard for the constitutional rights of African-Americans, by police departments in various American cities, including [Los Angeles](218985.md), [New York](255413.md), [Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Police_Department) and [Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_Police_Department).[^cite_note-\:42-45][^cite_note-46][^cite_note-47][^cite_note-\:222-48][^cite_note-49][^cite_note-50]

A report by the [National Registry of Exonerations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Registry_of_Exonerations) found that, as of August 2022, [African Americans](2154.md) make up 13.6% of the U.S. population but 53% of exonerations, and that they were seven times more likely to be falsely convicted, compared to White Americans.[^cite_note-51]

### Education

See also: [Discrimination in education § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discrimination_in_education#United_States), [School segregation in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_segregation_in_the_United_States), [School integration in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_integration_in_the_United_States), and [Racism in United States college fraternities and sororities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism_in_United_States_college_fraternities_and_sororities)

In 1954, *[Brown vs. the Board of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_v._Board_of_Education)* ruled that [integrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desegregation), equal schools be accessible to all children, unbiased to skin color. Currently, in the United States, not all state funded schools are equally funded. Schools are funded by the "federal, state, and local governments," while "states play a large and increasing role in education funding."[^cite_note-\:11-52] "[Property taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Property_tax) support most of the funding that local government provides for education."[^cite_note-\:11-52] Schools located in lower income areas receive a lower level of funding, and schools located in higher income areas receiving greater funding for education, all based on property taxes. The [U.S. Department of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Education) reports that "many high-poverty schools receive less than their fair share of state and local funding, leaving students in high-poverty schools with fewer resources than schools attended by their wealthier peers."[^cite_note-\:13-53] The U.S. Department of Education also reports this fact affects "more than 40% of low-income schools."[^cite_note-\:13-53] Children of color are much more likely to suffer from poverty than white children.

The phrase "brown paper bag test," also known as a [paper bag party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paper_bag_party), along with the "ruler test" refers to a ritual once practiced by certain African-American sororities and fraternities who would not let anyone into the group whose skin tone was darker than a paper bag.[^cite_note-Kerr-54] [Spike Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spike_Lee)'s film, *[School Daze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_Daze),* satirized this practice at historically Black colleges and universities.[^cite_note-55] Along with the "paper bag test," guidelines for acceptance among the lighter ranks included the "comb test" and "pencil test," which tested the coarseness of one's hair, and the "flashlight test," which tested a person's profile to make sure their features measured up or were close enough to those of the Caucasian race.[^cite_note-Kerr-54]

The curriculum in U.S. schools has also included racial discrimination against non-white Americans, including Native Americans, African Americans, Mexican Americans, and Asian Americans. Particularly in the 19th and early 20th centuries, school textbooks and other educational materials often portrayed black people as simple, irresponsible, and in many cases in situations of suffering that were implicitly their own fault (rather than caused by slavery and other pressures). African Americans were also portrayed as worthless and their suffering as normal, such as in a poem about "ten little black boys" dying one by one, published as a counting exercise for children from 1875 until the mid-20th century. Historian Carter J. Woodson analyzed the American curriculum as completely devoid of any reference to the merits of African Americans in the early 20th century. Based on his observations at the time, he wrote that American students, including African-American students who passed through the United States education system, concluded that blacks had no significant history and had made no contribution to human civilization.[^cite_note-56][^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58]

#### Curriculum

See also: [White supremacy in U.S. school curriculum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_supremacy_in_U.S._school_curriculum)

The curriculum in U.S. schools has also contained racism against non-white Americans, including Native Americans, [Black Americans](2154.md), [Mexican Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Americans), and Asian Americans.[^cite_note-\:20-59][^cite_note-\:10-60] Particularly, during the 19th and early 20th centuries, school textbooks and other teaching materials emphasized the biological and social inferiority of Black Americans, consistently portraying Black people as simple, irresponsible, and oftentimes, in situations of suffering that were implied to be their fault (and not the effects of slavery and other oppression).[^cite_note-\:20-59][^cite_note-\:10-60] Black Americans were also depicted as expendable and their suffering as commonplace, as evidenced by a poem about "Ten Little Nigger Boys" dying off, one-by-one, that was circulated as a children's counting exercise from 1875 to the mid-1900s.[^cite_note-\:10-60] Historian [Carter G. Woodson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carter_G._Woodson) analyzed American curriculum as completely lacking any mention of Black Americans' merits in the early 20th century. Based on his observations of the time, he wrote that American students, including Black students who went through U.S. schooling, would come out believing that Black people had no significant history and had contributed nothing to human civilization.[^cite_note-61]

School curriculum, often, implicitly and explicitly upheld white people as the superior race and marginalized the contributions and perspectives of non-white peoples, as if they were (or are) not as important.[^cite_note-Mills,_Charles_W\._1994-62] In the 19th century, a significant number of students were taught that [Adam and Eve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_and_Eve) were white, and the other races evolved from their various descendants, growing further and further away from the original white standard.[^cite_note-\:20-59] In addition, whites were also fashioned as the capable caretakers of other races, namely Black and Native people, who could not take care of themselves.[^cite_note-\:20-59] This concept was at odds with the violence white Americans had committed against indigenous and Black peoples, but it was coupled with soft language that, for example, defended these acts. Mills (1994) cites the narrative about Europeans' "discovery" of a "[New World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_World)," despite the people who already inhabited it and its subsequent "colonization," instead of conquest, as examples. He maintains that these word choices constitute a cooptation of history by white people, who have used it to their advantage.[^cite_note-Mills,_Charles_W\._1994-62]

### Health

Main article: [Race and health in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_and_health_in_the_United_States)

See also: [Medical Apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_Apartheid), [Medical racism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_racism_in_the_United_States), and [Unethical human experimentation in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unethical_human_experimentation_in_the_United_States)

A 2019 review of the literature in the *[Annual Review of Public health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annual_Review_of_Public_health)* found that [structural racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institutional_racism), [cultural racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_racism), and individual-level discrimination are "a fundamental cause of adverse health outcomes for racial/ethnic minorities and racial/ethnic inequities in health."[^cite_note-63]

Studies have argued that there are racial disparities in how the media and politicians act, when they are faced with cases of drug addiction in which the victims are primarily Black, rather than white, citing the examples of how society responded differently to the [crack epidemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crack_epidemic_in_the_United_States) than the [opioid epidemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opioid_epidemic_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65]

There are major [racial differences in access to health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_and_health) as well as major racial differences in the quality of the health care, which is provided to people. A study published in the *American Journal of Public Health* estimated that: "over 886,000 deaths could have been prevented, from 1991 to 2000, if African Americans had received the same quality of care as whites." The key differences that they cited were lack of insurance, inadequate [insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance), poor service, and reluctance to seek care.[^cite_note-66] A history of government-sponsored experimentation, such as the notorious [Tuskegee Syphilis Study](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuskegee_Syphilis_Study), has left a legacy of African American distrust of the medical system.[^cite_note-67]

Inequalities in health care may also reflect a [systemic bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Systemic_bias) in the way in which medical procedures and treatments are prescribed to members of different ethnic groups. A [University of Edinburgh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Edinburgh) Professor of Public Health, Raj Bhopal, writes that the history of [racism in science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_racism) and medicine shows that people and institutions behave according to the ethos of their times, and he also warns of dangers that need to be avoided in the future.[^cite_note-68] Nancy Krieger, a [Harvard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard) Professor of Social Epidemiology, contended that much modern research supported the assumptions which were needed to justify racism. She wrote that racism underlies unexplained inequities in health care, including treatments for [heart disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiovascular_disease),[^cite_note-69] [renal failure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kidney_failure),[^cite_note-70] [bladder cancer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bladder_cancer),[^cite_note-71] and [pneumonia](52135.md).[^cite_note-72] Bhopal writes that these inequalities have been documented in various studies, and there are consistent findings that Black Americans receive less health care than white Americans—particularly where this involves expensive new technology.[^cite_note-73] The University of Michigan Health study found, in 2010, that black patients in pain clinics received 50% of the amount of drugs that other patients who were white received.[^cite_note-74] Black pain in medicine links to the racial disparities between pain management and racial bias on behalf of the health professional. In 2011, Vermont organizers took a proactive stand against racism in their communities to defeat the biopolitical struggles faced on a daily basis. The first and only universal health care law was passed in the state.[^cite_note-75]

Two local governments in the US have issued declarations, stating that racism constitutes a [public health emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_health_emergency_\(United_States\)): the [Milwaukee County, Wisconsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_County,_Wisconsin) executive in May 2019, and the [Cleveland City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland_City_Council), in June 2020.[^cite_note-76][^cite_note-77]

### Housing and land

Main article: [Housing discrimination in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_discrimination_in_the_United_States)

A 2014 meta-analysis found extensive evidence of racial discrimination in the American housing market.[^cite_note-RichExperiments-78] Minority applicants for housing needed to make many more inquiries to view properties.[^cite_note-RichExperiments-78] Geographical steering of African Americans in US housing remains significant.[^cite_note-RichExperiments-78] A 2003 study found "evidence that agents interpret an initial housing request as an indication of a customer's preferences, but also are more likely to withhold a house from all customers when it is in an integrated suburban neighborhood ([redlining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redlining)). Moreover, agents' marketing efforts increase with asking price for white, but not for black, customers; blacks are more likely than whites to see houses in suburban, integrated areas ([steering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_steering)); and the houses agents show are more likely to deviate from the initial request when the customer is black than when the customer is white. These three findings are consistent with the possibility that agents act upon the belief that some types of transactions are relatively unlikely for black customers (statistical discrimination)."[^cite_note-79] Historically, there was extensive and long-lasting racial discrimination against African Americans in the housing and mortgage markets in the United States,[^cite_note-80][^cite_note-81] as well as discrimination against Black farmers whose numbers massively declined in post-WWII America due to anti-Black local and federal policies.[^cite_note-82] According to a 2019 analysis by University of Pittsburgh economists, Blacks faced a two-fold penalty due to the racially segregated housing market: rental prices increased in blocks when they underwent racial transition whereas home values declined in neighborhoods that Blacks moved into.[^cite_note-83]

A 2017 paper by Troesken and Walsh found that pre-20th century cities "created and sustained residential segregation through private norms and vigilante activity." However, "when these private arrangements began to break down during the early 1900s" whites started "lobbying municipal governments for segregation ordinances." As a result, cities passed ordinances which "prohibited members of the majority racial group on a given city block from selling or renting property to members of another racial group" between 1909 and 1917.[^cite_note-84]

A 2017 study by Federal Reserve Bank of Chicago economists found that the practice of [redlining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redlining)—the practice whereby banks discriminated against the inhabitants of certain neighborhoods—had a persistent adverse impact on the neighborhoods, with redlining affecting homeownership rates, home values and credit scores in 2010.[^cite_note-85][^cite_note-\:16-86] Since many African Americans could not access conventional home loans, they had to turn to predatory lenders (who charged high interest rates).[^cite_note-\:16-86] Due to lower homeownership rates, slumlords were able to rent out apartments that would otherwise be owned.[^cite_note-\:16-86] A 2019 analysis estimated that predatory housing contracts targeting African Americans in Chicago in the 1950s and 1960s cost Black families between \$3 billion and \$4 billion in wealth.[^cite_note-87]

### Labor market

Main articles: [Employment discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Employment_discrimination) and [Occupational segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupational_segregation)

Several meta-analyses find extensive evidence of ethnic and racial discrimination in hiring in the American labor market.[^cite_note-RichExperiments-78][^cite_note-\:22-88][^cite_note-\:8-89][^cite_note-90] A 2017 meta-analysis found "no change in the levels of discrimination against African Americans since 1989, although we do find some indication of declining discrimination against Latinos."[^cite_note-91] A 2016 meta-analysis of 738 correspondence tests – tests where identical CVs for stereotypically Black and white names were sent to employers – in 43 separate studies conducted in OECD countries between 1990 and 2015 finds that there is extensive racial discrimination in hiring decisions in Europe and North America.[^cite_note-\:22-88] These correspondence tests showed that equivalent minority candidates need to send around 50% more applications to be invited for an interview than majority candidates.[^cite_note-\:22-88][^cite_note-92] A study which examined the job applications of actual people who were provided with identical résumés and similar interview training showed that African-American applicants with no criminal record were offered jobs at a rate as low as white applicants who had criminal records.[^cite_note-93] A 2018 National Bureau of Economic Research paper found evidence of racial bias in how CVs were evaluated.[^cite_note-94] A 2020 study revealed that discrimination not only exists against minorities in callback rates in audit studies, it also increases in severity after the callbacks in terms of job offers.[^cite_note-95]

Research suggests that light-skinned African American women have higher salaries and greater job satisfaction than dark-skinned women.[^cite_note-Hunter-96] Being "too black" has recently been acknowledged by the U.S. Federal courts in an employment discrimination case under Title VII of the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964). In *Etienne v. Spanish Lake Truck & Casino Plaza, LLC* the [United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Fifth_Circuit), determined that an employee who was told on several occasions that her manager thought she was "too black" to do various tasks, found that the issue of the employee's skin color, rather than race, itself, played a key role in an employer's decision to keep the employee from advancing.[^cite_note-97] A 2018 study uncovered evidence which suggests that immigrants with darker skin colors are discriminated against.[^cite_note-98]

### Media

See also: [Cyber racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyber_racism), [Racial bias in criminal news in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_bias_in_criminal_news_in_the_United_States), [Racial stereotyping in advertising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_stereotyping_in_advertising), and [Representation of African Americans in media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representation_of_African_Americans_in_media)

A 2017 report by Travis L. Dixon, professor at the University of Illinois at Urbana-Champaign, found that major media outlets tended to portray Black families as dysfunctional and dependent, while portraying white families as stable. These portrayals included suggestions that poverty and welfare are primarily Black issues. According to Dixon, this can reduce public support for social safety programs and lead to stricter welfare requirements.[^cite_note-99][^cite_note-100]

African Americans who possess a lighter skin complexion and "European features," such as lighter eyes, and smaller noses and lips have more opportunities in the media industry. For example, film producers hire lighter-skinned African Americans more often, television producers choose lighter-skinned cast members, and magazine editors choose African American models that resemble European features.[^cite_note-101] A content analysis conducted by Scott and Neptune (1997) shows that less than one percent of advertisements in major magazines featured African American models. When African Americans did appear in advertisements, they were mainly portrayed as athletes, entertainers, or unskilled laborers. In addition, seventy percent of the advertisements that feature animal print included African American women. Animal print reinforces the stereotypes that African Americans are animalistic in nature, sexually active, less educated, have lower income, and extremely concerned with personal appearances.[^cite_note-Pious-102] Concerning African American males in the media, darker-skinned men are more likely to be portrayed as violent or more threatening, influencing the public perception of African American men. Since dark-skinned males are more likely to be linked to crime and misconduct, many people develop preconceived notions about the characteristics of Black men.[^cite_note-Hall-103]

During and after slavery, minstrel shows were a very popular form of theater that involved white and Black people in [Blackface](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackface), portraying Black people while doing demeaning things. The actors painted their faces with Black paint and overlined their lips with bright red lipstick, to exaggerate and make fun of Black people.[^cite_note-104] When minstrel shows died out and television became popular, Black actors were rarely hired, and when they were, they had very specific roles. These roles included being servants, slaves, idiots, and criminals.[^cite_note-105]

### Politics

See also: [Identity politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identity_politics), [Nativism (politics) § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_\(politics\)#United_States), [Nativism in United States politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_in_United_States_politics), [Politics of the United States § Race and ethnicity](32021.md#Race_and_ethnicity), [Politics of the Southern United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_the_Southern_United_States), [Conservatism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States), [Radical right (United States)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radical_right_\(United_States\)), [Xenophobia § United States](34141.md#United_States), [Xenophobia in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xenophobia_in_the_United_States), and [Criticism of welfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_welfare)

Politically, the "[winner-takes-all](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winner-takes-all_voting)" structure of the [electoral college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_electoral_college) benefits white representation.[^cite_note-\:1-106] This has been described as structural bias and often leads voters of color to feel [politically alienated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_alienation) and therefore, not to vote. The lack of representation in Congress has also led to lower voter turnout.[^cite_note-\:1-106] As of 2016, African Americans only made up 8.7% of Congress, and Latinos 7%.[^cite_note-107]

[Voter ID laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_ID_laws_in_the_United_States) have brought on accusations of racial discrimination. In a 2014 review by the [Government Accountability Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_Accountability_Office) of the academic literature, three studies out of five found that voter ID laws reduced minority turnout, whereas two studies found no significant impact.[^cite_note-RichExperiments-78] Disparate impact may also be reflected in access to information about voter ID laws. A 2015 experimental study found that election officials queried about voter ID laws are more likely to respond to emails from a non-Latino white name (70.5% response rate) than a Latino name (64.8% response rate), though response accuracy was similar, across groups.[^cite_note-108] Studies have also analyzed racial differences in ID requests rates. A 2012 study in the city of Boston found that Black and Hispanic voters were more likely to be asked for ID, during the 2008 election. According to exit polls, 23% of whites, 33% of Blacks, and 38% of Hispanics were asked for ID, though this effect is partially attributed to Black and Hispanics preferring non-peak voting hours, when election officials inspected a greater portion of IDs. Precinct differences also confound the data as Black and Hispanic voters tended to vote at Black and Hispanic-majority precincts.[^cite_note-109] A 2015 study found that turnout, among Blacks in Georgia, was generally higher, since the state began enforcing its strict voter ID law.[^cite_note-110] A 2016 study by [University of California, San Diego](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_California,_San_Diego) researchers found that voter ID laws "have a differentially negative impact on the turnout of Hispanics, Blacks, and mixed-race Americans in primaries and general elections."[^cite_note-111]

Research by University of Oxford economist Evan Soltas and Stanford political scientist David Broockman suggests that voters act upon racially discriminatory tastes.[^cite_note-112] A 2018 study in *[Public Opinion Quarterly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Opinion_Quarterly)* found that whites, in particular those who had racial resentment, largely attributed Obama's success among African-Americans to his race and not his characteristics as a candidate and the political preferences of African-Americans.[^cite_note-113] A 2018 study in the journal *[American Politics Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Politics_Research)* found that white voters tended to misperceive political candidates from racial minorities as being more ideologically extreme than objective indicators would suggest; this adversely affected the electoral chances for those candidates.[^cite_note-114] A 2018 study in the *[Journal of Politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Journal_of_Politics)* found that "when a white candidate makes vague statements, many \[nonblack] voters project their own policy positions onto the candidate, increasing support for the candidate. But they are less likely to extend black candidates the same courtesy... In fact, black male candidates who make ambiguous statements are actually punished for doing so by racially prejudiced voters."[^cite_note-115]

It is argued that the racial coding of concepts, like crime and welfare, has been used to strategically influence public political views. Racial coding is implicit; it incorporates racially primed language or imagery to allude to racial attitudes and thinking. For example, in the context of domestic policy, it is argued that [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) implied that linkages existed between concepts like "special interests" and "[big government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_government)" and ill-perceived minority groups in the 1980s, using the conditioned negativity which existed toward the minority groups to discredit certain policies and programs during campaigns. In a study which analyzes how political ads prime attitudes, Valentino compares the voting responses of participants after they are exposed to the narration of a George W. Bush advertisement which is paired with three different types of visuals which contain different embedded racial cues to create three conditions: neutral, race comparison, and undeserving Blacks. For example, as the narrator states "Democrats want to spend your tax dollars on wasteful government programs," the video shows an image of a Black woman and her child in an office setting. Valentino found that the undeserving Blacks condition produced the largest primed effect in racialized policies, like opposition to [affirmative action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affirmative_action) and welfare spending.[^cite_note-Valentino-116]

[Ian Haney López](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Haney_L%C3%B3pez), Professor of Law at the [University of California, Berkeley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_California,_Berkeley), refers to the phenomenon of racial coding as [dog-whistle politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dog-whistle_politics), which, he argues, has pushed middle class white Americans to vote against their economic self-interest to punish "undeserving minorities" which, they believe, are receiving too much public assistance at their expense. According to López, conservative middle-class whites, convinced that minorities are the enemy by powerful economic interests, supported politicians who promised to curb illegal immigration and crack down on crime, but inadvertently they also voted for policies that favor the extremely rich, such as slashing taxes for top income brackets, giving corporations more regulatory control over industry and financial markets, [busting unions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_busting), cutting pensions for future public employees, reducing funding for public schools, and retrenching the social welfare state. He argues that these same voters cannot link rising inequality which has impacted their lives to the policy agendas which they support, which resulted in a massive transfer of wealth to the top 1% of the population since the 1980s.[^cite_note-117]

A book released by the former attorney of [Donald Trump](4848272.md), [Michael Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Cohen_\(lawyer\)), in September 2020, *[Disloyal: A Memoir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disloyal:_A_Memoir)* described Trump as routinely referring to Black leaders of foreign nations with racial insults and that he was consumed with hatred for [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama). Cohen, in the book, explained that "as a rule, Trump expressed low opinions of all Black folks, from music to culture and politics".[^cite_note-118]

### Religion

Main article: [Racial segregation of churches in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation_of_churches_in_the_United_States)

See also: [Religion in the United States § Ethnicity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_the_United_States#Ethnicity), [Freedom of religion in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_religion_in_the_United_States), [History of religion in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_religion_in_the_United_States), [Anti-Catholicism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Catholicism_in_the_United_States), [Anti-Mormonism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Mormonism), [Antisemitism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_the_United_States), [History of antisemitism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_antisemitism_in_the_United_States), [List of antisemitic incidents in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_antisemitic_incidents_in_the_United_States), [List of attacks against African-American churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_attacks_against_African-American_churches), [List of attacks against Latter-day Saint churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_attacks_against_Latter-day_Saint_churches), [Lynching of American Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_of_American_Jews), [Religion of Black Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_of_Black_Americans), and [Religious discrimination in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_discrimination_in_the_United_States)

White [Christians](18337522.md) are consistently more likely than whites who are [religiously unaffiliated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion) to deny the existence of [structural racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Structural_racism).[^cite_note-119]

### Wealth

See also: [Wealth inequality in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wealth_inequality_in_the_United_States), [Social determinants of mental health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_determinants_of_mental_health), and [Social determinants of health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_determinants_of_health)

Large racial differentials in wealth remain in the United States: between whites and African Americans, the gap is a factor of twenty.[^cite_note-120] An analyst of the phenomenon, Thomas Shapiro, professor of law and social policy at [Brandeis University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brandeis_University) argues, "The [wealth gap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wealth_gap) is not just a story of merit and achievement, it's also a story of the historical legacy of race in the United States."[^cite_note-121] Differentials applied to the [Social Security Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Security_\(United_States\)) (which excluded agricultural workers, a sector which then included most black workers), rewards to military officers, and the educational benefits offered returning soldiers after [World War II](32927.md). Pre-existing disparities in wealth are exacerbated by tax policies that reward investment over waged income, subsidize mortgages, and subsidize private sector developers.[^cite_note-122]

Redlining intentionally excluded black Americans from accumulating intergenerational wealth.[^cite_note-123] The effects of this exclusion on black Americans' health continue to play out daily, generations later, in the same communities.[^cite_note-124] This is evident currently in the disproportionate effects that [COVID-19](63030231.md) has had on the same communities which the [HOLC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_Owners'_Loan_Corporation) (Home Owners' Loan Corporation) redlined in the 1930s. Research published in September 2020 overlaid maps of the highly affected COVID-19 areas with the HOLC maps, showing that those areas marked "risky" to lenders because they contained minority residents were the same neighborhoods most affected by COVID-19.[^cite_note-\:4-125] The [Centers for Disease Control](6811.md) (CDC) looks at inequities in the social determinants of health like concentrated poverty and healthcare access that are interrelated and influence health outcomes with regard to COVID-19 as well as quality of life in general for minority groups.[^cite_note-\:52-126] The CDC points to discrimination within health care, education, criminal justice, housing, and finance, direct results of systematically subversive tactics like redlining which led to chronic and toxic stress that shaped social and economic factors for minority groups, increasing their risk for COVID-19. Healthcare access is similarly limited by factors like a lack of public transportation, child care, and communication and language barriers which result from the spatial and economic isolation of minority communities from redlining.[^cite_note-\:52-126] Educational, income, and wealth gaps that result from this isolation mean that minority groups' limited access to the job market may force them to remain in fields that have a higher risk of exposure to the virus, without options to take time off. Finally, a direct result of redlining is the overcrowding of minority groups into neighborhoods that do not boast adequate housing to sustain burgeoning populations, leading to crowded conditions that make prevention strategies for COVID-19 nearly impossible to implement.[^cite_note-\:4-125][^cite_note-Myers,_JoAnne_2020-127][^cite_note-128]

A 2014 meta-analysis of racial discrimination in product markets found extensive evidence of minority applicants being quoted higher prices for products.[^cite_note-RichExperiments-78]

Historically, African-Americans have faced discrimination in terms of getting access to credit.[^cite_note-129]

## African Americans

Main article: [Racism against African Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism_against_African_Americans)

Further information: [African-American history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_history), [Slavery in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States), and [Slavery in the colonial history of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_colonial_history_of_the_United_States)

### Antebellum period



Scars on an enslaved man, Peter, April 2, 1863, [Baton Rouge, Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baton_Rouge,_Louisiana)

Further information: [Antebellum South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antebellum_South)

Between 1626 and 1860, the [Atlantic slave trade](49005.md) brought more than 470,000 enslaved Africans to what is now the United States.[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131] White (European) Americans who participated in the slave industry tried to justify their economic exploitation of [Black people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_people) by creating a ["scientific" theory of white superiority and Black inferiority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_racism).[^cite_note-Boggs-132] One such slave owner was [Thomas Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson), and it was his call for science to determine the obvious "inferiority" of Blacks that is regarded as "an extremely important stage in the evolution of scientific racism."[^cite_note-133] He concluded that Blacks were "inferior to the whites in the endowments of body and mind."[^cite_note-134]

After the importation of [slaves into the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States) was outlawed by federal law from 1808, the [domestic slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States#Domestic_slave_trade_and_forced_migration) was expanded in an attempt to replace it.[^cite_note-CUP-135] Maryland and Virginia, for example, would "export" their surplus slaves to the South. These sales of slaves broke up many families, with historian [Ira Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ira_Berlin) writing that whether slaves were directly uprooted or lived in fear that they or their families would be involuntarily moved, "the massive deportation traumatized black people".[^cite_note-136]

During the 1820s and 1830s, the [American Colonization Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Colonization_Society) established the colony of [Liberia](17791.md) and persuaded thousands of free Black Americans to move there because many members of the white elite both in the North and the South saw them as a problem to be got rid of.

[Abraham Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln), who opposed the spread of slavery, spoke out for white superiority before the Civil War.[^cite_note-137] Lincoln said during the Fourth [Lincoln-Douglas debate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln-Douglas_debates) held in Charleston, Illinois, on September 18, 1858: "I am not, nor ever have been, in favor of bringing about in any way the social and political equality of the white and black races, \[applause]-that I am not nor ever have been in favor of making voters or jurors of negroes, nor of qualifying them to hold office, nor to intermarry with white people; and I will say in addition to this that there is a physical difference between the white and black races which I believe will forever forbid the two races living together on terms of social and political equality. And inasmuch as they cannot so live, while they do remain together there must be the position of superior and inferior, and I as much as any other man am in favor of having the superior position assigned to the white race."[^cite_note-138]

In 1862-1865, during and immediately after the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War), all four million enslaved African Americans were set free. Major legal actions included [President Lincoln](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln)'s [Emancipation Proclamation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emancipation_Proclamation) which came into effect on January 1, 1863, and the [Thirteenth Amendment to the United States Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution) which finally abolished slavery in December 1865.[^cite_note-139]

### From the Reconstruction Era to World War II

Main article: [Nadir of American race relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nadir_of_American_race_relations)

See also: [List of expulsions of African Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_expulsions_of_African_Americans), [Lynching in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States), and [Mass racial violence in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_racial_violence_in_the_United_States)



A group of white men pose for a 1919 photograph as they stand over the body of the Black lynching victim Will Brown before they decide to mutilate and burn them during the [Omaha race riot of 1919](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omaha_race_riot_of_1919) in [Omaha, Nebraska](46159.md). Photographs and postcards of lynchings were popular souvenirs in the U.S.

[^cite_note-140]

After the [Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War), the [Reconstruction Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstruction_Era) was characterized by the passage of federal legislation which was designed to protect the rights of the formerly enslaved people, including the [Civil Rights Act of 1866](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1866) and the [Civil Rights Act of 1875](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1875). The [Fourteenth amendment](31666.md) granted full citizenship to African Americans and the [Fifteenth amendment](31667.md) guaranteed the [voting rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_rights) of African-American men (see [Reconstruction Amendments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstruction_Amendments)).

Despite this, [white supremacists](33178.md) came to power in all Southern states, by intimidating Black voters with the assistance of terrorist groups like the [Ku Klux Klan](16779.md), the [Red Shirts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Shirts_\(Southern_United_States\)) and the [White League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_League). "[Black Codes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Codes_\(United_States\))" and [Jim Crow laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow_laws) deprived African Americans of voting rights and other civil liberties by instituting systemic and discriminatory policies of unequal [racial segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation).[^cite_note-141] Segregated facilities extended from white-only schools to white-only graveyards.[^cite_note-142] [Anti-miscegenation laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-miscegenation_laws_in_the_United_States) forbade marriage and even sex between whites and non-whites.[^cite_note-143]

The new century saw a hardening of [institutionalized racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institutionalized_racism) and legal discrimination against citizens of African descent in the United States. Throughout the post-Civil War period, [racial stratification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_stratification) was informally and systemically enforced, to solidify the pre-existing social order. Although their vote was guaranteed by the 15th Amendment, [poll taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poll_tax_\(United_States\)), pervasive acts of [terrorism](30636.md) such as [lynchings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States) (often perpetrated by [hate groups](437067.md) such as the [Ku Klux Klan](16779.md)), and discriminatory laws such as [grandfather clauses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grandfather_clauses) kept Black Americans [disenfranchised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disfranchisement_after_the_Reconstruction_era) in most Southern states. In response to [de jure](43594.md) racism, protest and lobbyist groups emerged, most notably, the [NAACP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAACP) (National Association for the Advancement of Colored People) in 1909.[^cite_note-144]

This era is sometimes referred to as the [nadir of American race relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nadir_of_American_race_relations) because racism, [segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation_in_the_United_States), [racial discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_discrimination), and expressions of [white supremacy](33178.md) all increased. So did anti-Black violence, including [race riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_racial_violence_in_the_United_States) such as the [Atlanta race riot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta_race_riot) of 1906, the [East St. Louis massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_St._Louis_massacre) of 1917, the [Elaine massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elaine_massacre) of 1919, the [Ocoee massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ocoee_massacre) of 1920, the [Tulsa race massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulsa_race_massacre) of 1921, the [Perry massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perry_massacre) of 1922, and the [Rosewood massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosewood_massacre) of 1923. The Atlanta riot was characterized as a "racial massacre of negroes" by the French newspaper *[Le Petit Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Petit_Journal_\(newspaper\))*.[^cite_note-145] The Charleston *[News and Courier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Post_and_Courier)* wrote in response to the Atlanta riots: "Separation of the races is the only radical solution of the negro problem in this country. There is nothing new about it. It was the Almighty who established the bounds of the habitation of the races. The negroes were brought here by compulsion; they should be induced to leave here by persuasion."[^cite_note-146]

Additionally, racism, which had previously been considered a problem which primarily existed in the [Southern states](179553.md), burst onto the nation's consciousness following the [Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_\(African_American\)), the relocation of millions of African Americans from their roots in the rural Southern states to the industrial centers of the North and West between 1910 and 1970.



White tenants seeking to prevent Blacks from moving into the [housing project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_housing_in_Detroit) erected this sign. Detroit, 1942.

Throughout this period, racial tensions exploded, most violently in Chicago, and [lynchings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States)—mob-directed hangings, usually racially motivated—increased dramatically in the 1920s. Urban riots—whites attacking Blacks—became a northern and western problem.[^cite_note-147] Many whites defended their space with violence, intimidation, or legal tactics toward African Americans, while many other whites migrated to more racially homogeneous suburban or exurban regions, a process known as [white flight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_flight).[^cite_note-Migration-148] Racially restrictive housing covenants were ruled unenforceable under the [14th Amendment](31666.md) in the 1948 landmark [Supreme Court](31737.md) case *[Shelley v. Kraemer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shelley_v._Kraemer)*.[^cite_note-149]

[Elected in 1912](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1912_United_States_presidential_election), President [Woodrow Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson) [authorized the practice of racial segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_and_race#Segregating_the_federal_bureaucracy) throughout the federal government's bureaucracy.[^cite_note-150] In [World War I](4764461.md), Blacks who served in the [United States Armed Forces](32212.md) served in [segregated units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation_in_the_United_States_Armed_Forces). Black soldiers were often poorly trained and equipped, and they were often put on the frontlines and forced to go on [suicide missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suicide_mission). The U.S. military was still heavily segregated during [World War II](32927.md). In addition, no African-American was awarded the [Medal of Honor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medal_of_Honor) during the war, and sometimes, Black soldiers who traveled on trains had to give their seats up to Nazi prisoners of war.[^cite_note-151]

### From World War II to the Civil Rights Movement

Further information: [Civil rights movement (1896–1954)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_rights_movement_\(1896%E2%80%931954\))



Due to threats and violence against her, U.S. Marshals escorted 6-year-old [Ruby Bridges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruby_Bridges) to and from the previously whites only [William Frantz Elementary School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Frantz_Elementary_School) in New Orleans, 1960. As soon as Bridges entered the school, white parents pulled their children out.

The [Jim Crow laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow_laws) were state and local laws which were enacted in the Southern and [border states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_states_\(Civil_War\)) and enforced between 1876 and 1965. They mandated "[separate but equal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separate_but_equal)" status for Blacks. In reality, this led to treatment and accommodations that were almost always inferior to those which were provided to whites. The most important laws required that public schools, public places, and public transportation, like trains and buses, have separate facilities for whites and Blacks. State-sponsored school segregation was declared unconstitutional by the [Supreme Court of the United States](31737.md) in 1954 in *[Brown v. Board of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_v._Board_of_Education)*. One of the first federal court cases which challenged segregation in schools was [Mendez v. Westminster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mendez_v._Westminster) in 1946.

By the 1950s, the [civil rights movement](49001.md) was gaining momentum. Membership in the NAACP increased in states across the U.S. Notable acts of anti-Black violence that sparked public outrage included the 1955 lynching of 14-year-old [Emmett Till](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmett_Till) and the 1963 assassination of civil rights activist and [NAACP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAACP) member [Medgar Evers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medgar_Evers) by a member of the [White Citizens' Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Citizens'_Council). In both cases, the perpetrators were able to evade conviction with the help of [all-white juries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_discrimination_in_jury_selection). In the 1963 [16th Street Baptist Church bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16th_Street_Baptist_Church_bombing), [Ku Klux Klansmen](16779.md) killed four Black girls, aged 11 to 14.[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153]

In response to heightening discrimination and violence, non-violent acts of protest began to occur. The [Greensboro sit-ins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greensboro_sit-ins), starting in February 1960, contributed to the formation of the [Student Nonviolent Coordinating Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_Nonviolent_Coordinating_Committee). After many [sit-ins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sit-in) and other non-violent protests, including marches and boycotts, places began to agree to desegregate.[^cite_note-154]



[Rosa Parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosa_Parks) being fingerprinted on February 22, 1956, after being arrested for not giving up her seat on the bus to a white person



[Bayard Rustin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayard_Rustin) (left) and [Cleveland Robinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland_Robinson) (right), organizers of the March, on August 7, 1963

The [March on Washington for Jobs and Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_on_Washington_for_Jobs_and_Freedom) on August 28, 1963, with an estimated 250,000 Black and white participants, at which [Martin Luther King Jr.](20076.md) delivered his historic "[I Have a Dream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_Have_a_Dream)" speech, helped facilitate the passage of the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964) and the [Voting Rights Act of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act_of_1965). In *[Loving v. Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loving_v._Virginia)* (1967), the Supreme Court declared [anti-miscegenation laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-miscegenation_law) unconstitutional.[^cite_note-Anti-miscegenation-155]

Segregation continued even after the demise of the Jim Crow laws. Data on house prices and attitudes towards integration suggest that in the mid-20th century, segregation was a product of collective actions taken by whites to exclude Blacks from their neighborhoods.[^cite_note-vigdor-156] Segregation also took the form of [redlining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redlining), the practice of denying or increasing the cost of services, such as banking, insurance, access to jobs,[^cite_note-157] access to health care,[^cite_note-158] or even supermarkets[^cite_note-159] to residents in certain, often racially determined,[^cite_note-eastny-160] areas. Although in the U.S. informal discrimination and segregation have always existed, redlining began with the [National Housing Act of 1934](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Housing_Act_of_1934), which established the [Federal Housing Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Housing_Administration) (FHA). The practice was fought first through passage of the [Fair Housing Act of 1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_Housing_Act_of_1968) (which prevents redlining when the criteria for redlining are based on race, religion, gender, familial status, disability, or ethnic origin), and later through the [Community Reinvestment Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_Reinvestment_Act) of 1977, which requires banks to apply the same lending criteria in all communities.[^cite_note-161] Although redlining is illegal some argue that it continues to exist in other forms.

Up until the 1940s, the full revenue potential of what was called "the Negro market" was largely ignored by white-owned manufacturers in the U.S., with advertising focused on whites.[^cite_note-162] Blacks, including Olympic champion [Jesse Owens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesse_Owens),[^cite_note-espn1-163][^cite_note-164] were also denied commercial deals. Famous Blacks like Owens and [Hattie McDaniel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hattie_McDaniel) had to suffer humiliating treatment even at events celebrating their achievements.[^cite_note-schwartz-165][^cite_note-LA_segregation-166]

As the civil rights movement and the dismantling of Jim Crow laws in the 1950s and 1960s deepened existing racial tensions in much of the Southern U.S., a Republican Party electoral strategy – the [Southern strategy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_strategy) – was enacted to increase political support among white voters in the South by appealing to racism against African Americans.[^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168] Republican politicians such as presidential candidate [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon) and Senator [Barry Goldwater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Goldwater) developed strategies that successfully contributed to the political realignment of many white, conservative voters in the South who had traditionally supported the Democratic Party rather than the Republican Party.[^cite_note-Apple-169] Nixon and [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) exhibited racial prejudice toward African people in a recorded phone conversation in 1971, and in another conversation with White House aids Nixon commented on African Americans: "We're going to (place) more of these little Negro bastards on the welfare rolls at \$2,400 a family".[^cite_note-170]

### From the 1970s to the 2000s



The [Emanuel African Methodist Episcopal Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emanuel_African_Methodist_Episcopal_Church) where nine Black church-goers, including the pastor, were killed by [a white man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dylann_Roof) in the 2015 [Charleston church shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charleston_church_shooting). The church, founded in 1817, is the oldest [AME](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Methodist_Episcopal_Church) church in the South.

While substantial gains were made in the succeeding decades through middle class advancement and public employment, [Black poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_poverty) and lack of education continued in the context of de-industrialization.[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-Takaki-CivRights-172]

From 1981 to 1997, the [United States Department of Agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Agriculture) discriminated against tens of thousands of Black American farmers, denying loans that were provided to white farmers in similar circumstances. The discrimination was the subject of the *[Pigford v. Glickman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pigford_v._Glickman)* lawsuit brought by members of the [National Black Farmers Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Black_Farmers_Association), which resulted in two settlement agreements of \$1.06 billion in 1999 and of \$1.25 billion in 2009.[^cite_note-173]

Numerous authors, academics, and historians have asserted that the [war on drugs](1181646.md) has been racially and politically motivated. Continuing the "tough on crime" policies and rhetoric of earlier politicians, President [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) announced his administration's [war on drugs](1181646.md) in October 1982.[^cite_note-174] A few years later, the [crack epidemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crack_epidemic) spread across the country in the mid 1980s, leading Congress to pass the [Anti-Drug Abuse Act of 1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Drug_Abuse_Act_of_1986). Under these sentencing guidelines, five grams of [crack cocaine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crack_cocaine), often sold by and to African-Americans, carried a mandatory five-year prison sentence. However, for powder cocaine, often sold by and to white Americans, it would take one hundred times that amount, or 500 grams, for the same sentence, leading many to criticize the law as discriminatory. The 100\:1 sentencing disparity was reduced to 18\:1 in 2010 by the [Fair Sentencing Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_Sentencing_Act).[^cite_note-175]

In addition to discriminatory sentencing for drug-related offenses, many African-Americans have gone as far as accusing the U.S. government of deliberately flooding their communities with drugs.[^cite_note-176] It has been demonstrated that government officials, including the [CIA](5183633.md) and Reagan administration officials linked to the [Iran-Contra scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran-Contra_scandal), were aware of [drug trafficking by the Nicaraguan Contras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA_involvement_in_Contra_cocaine_trafficking), and collaborated with and protected Contra members who were known drug traffickers.[^cite_note-177] In 1996, the *[San Jose Mercury News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Jose_Mercury_News)* ran a series of articles by investigative journalist [Gary Webb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Webb) which linked the CIA-backed [Contras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicaraguan_Contras) to the origins of the crack epidemic, although this was rejected by separate investigations by *[The Los Angeles Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Los_Angeles_Times)*, *[The Washington Post](102226.md)* and *[The New York Times](30680.md)*.[^cite_note-178]

During the 1980s and 1990s, a number of riots occurred that were related to longstanding racial tensions between police and minority communities. One of these was the [1992 Los Angeles riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Los_Angeles_riots), after a nearly all-white jury acquitted four Los Angeles police officers for the beating of Black motorist [Rodney King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodney_King). [Khalil Gibran Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalil_Gibran_Muhammad), the Director of the Harlem-based [Schomburg Center for Research in Black Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schomburg_Center_for_Research_in_Black_Culture) has identified more than 100 instances of mass racial violence in the United States since 1935 and has noted that almost every instance was precipitated by a police incident.[^cite_note-179]

[Violence against Black churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_attacks_against_African-American_churches) has continued – 145 fires were set to [Black churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_church) around the [South](179553.md) in the 1990s,[^cite_note-180] and a [mass shooting in Charleston, South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charleston_church_shooting) was committed in 2015 at the historic [Mother Emanuel Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emanuel_African_Methodist_Episcopal_Church).[^cite_note-181]

### From 2008 to the present

See also: [Charleston church shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charleston_church_shooting), [United States racial unrest (2020–2023)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_racial_unrest_\(2020%E2%80%932023\)), and [2022 Buffalo shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Buffalo_shooting)



Reverend [Al Sharpton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Sharpton) speaking at the [Commitment March: Get Your Knee Off Our Necks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commitment_March:_Get_Your_Knee_Off_Our_Necks) in August 2020
