Some Americans saw the presidential election of [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama), who was the nation's first Black president, as a sign that the nation had entered a new, [post-racial era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-racial_America).[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-\:02-183] The election of President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) in 2016, who was a chief proponent of the [birther movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birther_movement) which is considered by many to be racist and has a history of speech and actions that have been [widely viewed as racist or racially charged](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_views_of_Donald_Trump), has been viewed by some commentators as a [racist backlash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_backlash#United_States) against the election of Barack Obama.[^cite_note-\:2-184] During the mid-2010s, American society has seen a resurgence of high levels of racism and discrimination. One new phenomenon has been the rise of the ["alt-right" movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alt-right): a [white nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_nationalism) coalition which seeks the expulsion of [sexual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_minority) and [racial minorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_group) from the United States.[^cite_note-185] Since the mid-2010s, the [Department of Homeland Security](58236.md) and the [Federal Bureau of Investigation](11127.md) have identified [white supremacist](33178.md) violence as the leading threat of [domestic terrorism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_terrorism_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187]

"Stopping" people\
you have nothing in common with

> &#x20;     And if you don't stop people that you've never seen before, that you have nothing in common with, your country is going to fail. ... This double-tailed monster \[of [green energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_energy) and [immigration](4599312.md)] destroys everything in its wake, and they cannot let that happen any longer. You're doing it because you want to be nice, you want to be politically correct, and you're destroying your heritage.

— President Donald Trump\
to the [UN General Assembly](31957.md), \
September 23, 2025

[^cite_note-TrumpUN_20250923-188]

Sociologist Russ Long stated in 2013 that there is now a more subtle racism that associates a specific race with a specific characteristic.[^cite_note-189] In a 1993 study conducted by Katz and Braly, it was presented that "blacks and whites hold a variety of stereotypes towards each other, often negative".[^cite_note-Leonard_1993-190] The Katz and Braley study also found that African-Americans and whites view the traits that they identify each other with as threatening, interracial communication between the two is likely to be "hesitant, reserved, and concealing".[^cite_note-Leonard_1993-190]

The [Black Lives Matter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Lives_Matter) movement started in 2013 after [the acquittal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trial_of_George_Zimmerman) of a man who had killed the African-American teen [Trayvon Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trayvon_Martin) in 2012.[^cite_note-191]

In August 2017, the [UN Committee on the Elimination of Racial Discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Convention_on_the_Elimination_of_All_Forms_of_Racial_Discrimination#Committee_on_the_Elimination_of_Racial_Discrimination) issued a rare warning to the population of the US and its leadership and it also urged them to "unequivocally and unconditionally" condemn racist speech and crime, following [violence in Charlottesville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlottesville_car_attack) during a [rally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unite_the_Right_rally) which was organized by [white nationalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_nationalism#United_States), [white supremacists](33178.md#United_States), [Klansmen](16779.md), [neo-Nazis](54361.md#United_States) and various [right-wing militias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_militia_movement) in August.[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193]

On May 25, 2020, [George Floyd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Floyd), a 46-year-old Black man, was [murdered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder_of_George_Floyd) by a white [Minneapolis Police Department](13087466.md) officer, Derek Michael Chauvin, who forced his knee on Floyd's neck for a total of [8 minutes and 46 seconds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8_minutes_and_46_seconds).[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195][^cite_note-9\:30_not_8\:46-201] Floyd's murder sparked a [wave of protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Floyd_protests) across the United States and worldwide[^cite_note-202] as well as reigniting the [BLM protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BLM_protests).

Most African Americans adults have said they have experienced racial discrimination according to [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center).[^cite_note-203]

## Native Americans

Main article: [Racism against Native Americans in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism_against_Native_Americans_in_the_United_States)

See also: [American Indian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Indian_Wars), [History of Native Americans in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Native_Americans_in_the_United_States), [List of Indian massacres in North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Indian_massacres_in_North_America), [Stereotypes of indigenous peoples of Canada and the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stereotypes_of_indigenous_peoples_of_Canada_and_the_United_States), [Thomas Jefferson and Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson_and_Native_Americans), [Indian Removal Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Removal_Act), [Choctaw Trail of Tears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choctaw_Trail_of_Tears), [California Genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Genocide), [Long Walk of the Navajo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_Walk_of_the_Navajo), [Comanche campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comanche_campaign), [Northern Cheyenne Exodus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Cheyenne_Exodus), [Potawatomi Trail of Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potawatomi_Trail_of_Death), [Sand Creek massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sand_Creek_massacre), [Trail of Tears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trail_of_Tears), [Timeline of Cherokee removal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Cherokee_removal), [Yavapai Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yavapai_Wars), [Indian removals in Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_removals_in_Indiana), and [Indian removals in Ohio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_removals_in_Ohio)



Members of the [Muscogee (Creek) Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muscogee_\(Creek\)_Nation) in Oklahoma around 1877

[Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas) have [inhabited the North American continent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Settlement_of_the_Americas) for at least 10,000 years, and [millions of Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_history_of_Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas) lived in the region composing the modern-day United States prior to [European colonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_colonization_of_the_Americas).[^cite_note-204] Both during and after the colonial period of American history, white settlers waged a [long series of wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Indian_Wars) against Native Americans with the aim of displacing them and colonizing their lands. Many Native Americans were [enslaved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_among_Native_Americans_in_the_United_States) as a result of these wars, while others were forcibly [assimilated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_assimilation) into the culture of the white settlers.[^cite_note-205]

During the 19th century, the desire to [forcibly remove certain Native American nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_removal) gained momentum. However, some Native Americans either chose to or were allowed to remain on their land and as a result, they avoided removal but thereafter, the federal government treated them in a racist manner. The Choctaws in [Mississippi](16949861.md) described their situation in 1849, "we have had our habitations torn down and burned, our fences destroyed, cattle turned into our fields and we ourselves have been scourged, manacled, fettered and otherwise personally abused, until by such treatment some of our best men have died."[^cite_note-peterson-206] According to Charles Hudson, [Joseph B. Cobb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_B._Cobb), who moved to Mississippi from Georgia, described the Choctaws as having "no nobility or virtue at all, and in some respects he found blacks, especially native Africans, to be more interesting and admirable, the red man's superior in every way. The Choctaw and Chickasaw, the tribes he knew best, were beneath contempt; that is, even worse than black slaves."[^cite_note-charles_hudson-207]



*[The Rescue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Rescue_\(sculpture\))* sculpture stood outside the U.S. Capitol building between 1853 and 1958. A work commissioned by the U.S. government, its sculptor [Horatio Greenough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horatio_Greenough) wrote that it was "to convey the idea of the triumph of the whites over the savage tribes".

[^cite_note-208]

In the 1800s, ideologies such as [manifest destiny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manifest_destiny), which held the view that the United States was destined to expand from coast to coast on the North American continent, fueled U.S. attacks against, and maltreatment of, Native Americans. In the years leading up to the [Indian Removal Act of 1830](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Removal_Act) there were many armed conflicts between white settlers and Native Americans.[^cite_note-209] A justification for the conquest and subjugation of indigenous people emanated from the stereotyped perception that Native Americans were "merciless Indian savages" (as described in the [United States Declaration of Independence](31874.md)).[^cite_note-210] Sam Wolfson in *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* writes, "The declaration's passage has often been cited as an encapsulation of the dehumanizing attitude toward indigenous Americans that the US was founded on."[^cite_note-211] Simon Moya-Smith, culture editor at *[Indian Country Today](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Country_Today)*, states, "Any holiday that would refer to my people in such a repugnant, racist manner is certainly not worth celebrating. \[July Fourth] is a day when we celebrate our resiliency, our culture, our languages, our children and we mourn the millions – literally millions – of indigenous people who have died as a consequence of American imperialism."[^cite_note-212]

In [Martin Luther King Jr.](20076.md)'s book *[Why We Can't Wait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Why_We_Can't_Wait)*, he wrote, "Our nation was born in genocide when it embraced the doctrine that the original American, the Indian, was an inferior race."[^cite_note-213] In 1861, residents of [Mankato, Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mankato,_Minnesota), formed the [Knights of the Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knights_of_the_Forest), with the goal of 'eliminating all Indians from Minnesota.' An egregious attempt occurred with the [California gold rush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_gold_rush), the first two years of which saw the death of thousands of Native Americans. Under [Mexican rule in California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alta_California), Indians were subjected to *de facto* enslavement under a system of [peonage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peon) by the white elite. While in 1850, California formally entered the [Union](3434750.md) as a [free state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_state_\(United_States\)), with respect to the issue of [slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States), the practice of Indian [indentured servitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indentured_servant) was not outlawed by the [California Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_State_Legislature) until 1863.[^cite_note-214] The 1864 deportation of the [Navajos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navajos) by the U.S. government occurred when 9,000 Navajos were forcibly relocated to an [internment camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internment_camp) in [Bosque Redondo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosque_Redondo),[^cite_note-Jaimes-215] where, under armed guards, up to 3,500 Navajo and [Mescalero Apache](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mescalero_Apache) men, women, and children died from starvation and disease over the next 4 years.[^cite_note-Jaimes-215]



Mass grave for the dead Lakota following the [Wounded Knee massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wounded_Knee_massacre). Eyewitness [American Horse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Horse), chief of the Oglala Lakota, stated, "A people's dream died there. It was a beautiful dream ... the nation's hope is broken and scattered. There is no center any longer, and the sacred tree is dead."

[^cite_note-216]

Native American nations on the plains in the west continued armed conflicts with the U.S. throughout the 19th century, through what were called generally [Indian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Indian_Wars).[^cite_note-217] Notable conflicts in this period include the [Dakota War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dakota_War_of_1862), [Great Sioux War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Sioux_War_of_1876), [Snake War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snake_War) and [Colorado War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_War). In the years leading up to the [Wounded Knee massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wounded_Knee_massacre) the U.S. government had continued to seize [Lakota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakota_people) lands. A [Ghost Dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghost_Dance) ritual on the Northern Lakota reservation at Wounded Knee, [South Dakota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Dakota), led to the U.S. Army's attempt to subdue the Lakota. The dance was part of a religion founded by [Wovoka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wovoka) that told of the return of the Messiah to relieve the suffering of Native Americans and promised that if they would live righteous lives and perform the Ghost Dance properly, the European American invaders would vanish, the bison would return, and the living and the dead would be reunited in an Edenic world.[^cite_note-218] On December 29, 1890, at Wounded Knee, gunfire erupted, and U.S. soldiers killed up to 300 Indians, mostly old men, women and children.[^cite_note-Wounded_Knee-219]

During the period surrounding the 1890 Wounded Knee Massacre, author [L. Frank Baum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L._Frank_Baum) wrote two editorials about Native Americans. Five days after the killing of the [Lakota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakota_people) [Sioux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sioux) [holy man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicine_man), [Sitting Bull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sitting_Bull), Baum wrote, "The proud spirit of the original owners of these vast prairies inherited through centuries of fierce and bloody wars for their possession, lingered last in the bosom of Sitting Bull. With his fall the nobility of the [Redskin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redskin) is extinguished, and what few are left are a pack of whining curs who lick the hand that smites them. The Whites, by the law of conquest, by a justice of civilization, are masters of the American continent, and the best safety of the frontier settlements will be secured by the total annihilation of the few remaining Indians. Why not annihilation? Their glory has fled, their spirit broken, their manhood effaced; better that they die than live the miserable wretches that they are."[^cite_note-editorials-220] Following the December 29, 1890, massacre, Baum wrote, "The *Pioneer* has before declared that our only safety depends upon the total extermination \[sic] of the Indians. Having wronged them for centuries we had better, in order to protect our civilization, follow it up by one more wrong and wipe these untamed and untamable creatures from the face of the earth. In this lies safety for our settlers and the soldiers who are under incompetent commands. Otherwise, we may expect future years to be as full of trouble with the redskins as those have been in the past."[^cite_note-editorials-220][^cite_note-221]

### Reservation marginalization

See also: [Native American reservations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_reservations)

Once their territories were incorporated into the United States, surviving Native Americans were denied equality before the law and often treated as wards of the state.[^cite_note-222]

Many Native Americans were moved to reservations—constituting 4% of U.S. territory. In a number of cases, treaties signed with Native Americans were violated. Tens of thousands of American Indians and Alaska Natives were forced to attend a [residential school system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_boarding_school) which sought to reeducate them in white-settler American values, culture, and economy.[^cite_note-223][^cite_note-224]

Further dispossession of various kinds continues into the present, although these current dispossessions, especially in terms of land, rarely make major news headlines in the country (e.g., the [Lenape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenape) people's recent fiscal troubles and subsequent land grab by the [State of New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_New_Jersey)), and sometimes even fail to make it to headlines in the localities in which they occur. Through concessions for industries such as oil, mining, and timber and through division of land from the [General Allotment Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dawes_Act) forward, these concessions have raised problems of consent, exploitation of low royalty rates, environmental injustice, and gross mismanagement of funds held in trust, resulting in the loss of \$10–40 billion.[^cite_note-225]

The [Worldwatch Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Worldwatch_Institute) notes that 317 reservations are threatened by environmental hazards, while [Western Shoshone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Shoshone) land has been subjected to more than 1,000 nuclear explosions.[^cite_note-226] However, the last known nuclear explosion testing in the United States occurred in September 1992.[^cite_note-227]

### American Indian boarding schools



Richard Henry Pratt founded the first Native American boarding school in 1879. The goal of these schools was to teach Native American students White ways of being through education which emphasized European cultural values and the superiority of White American ways of life.

[^cite_note-228]

[American Indian boarding schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Indian_boarding_schools), were established in the United States during the 19th and lasted through the mid-20th centuries with the primary objective of assimilating Native Americans into the dominant White American culture. The effect of these schools has been described as forced assimilation against Native peoples.[^cite_note-\:10-60][^cite_note-\:12-229] In these schools, Native children were prohibited from participating in any of their cultures' traditions, including speaking their own languages. Instead, they were required to speak English at all times and learn geography, science, and history (among other disciplines) as white Americans saw fit.[^cite_note-\:10-60][^cite_note-\:12-229] This meant learning a version of history that upheld whites' superiority and rightful "inheritance" of the lands of the United States, while Natives were relegated to a position of having to assimilate to white culture without ever truly being considered equals.[^cite_note-\:10-60]

### Current issues

While formal equality has been legally recognized, [American Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States), [Alaska Natives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska_Natives), [Native Hawaiians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Hawaiians), and [Pacific Islanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Islanders) remain among the most economically disadvantaged groups in the country, and according to national mental health studies, American Indians as a group tend to suffer from high levels of alcoholism, depression and suicide.[^cite_note-230] Native Americans are killed in police encounters at a higher rate than any other racial or ethnic group in the United States. Native Americans are killed by police at 3 times the rate of [White Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Americans) and 2.6 times the rate of [Black Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Americans), yet rarely do these deaths gain the national spotlight. The initial lack of media coverage and accountability has resulted in Indigenous-led movements such as Native Lives Matter and Missing and Murdered Indigenous Women, Girls, and People.[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232][^cite_note-Safety_and_Justice_Challenge-233]

Native Americans are disproportionately represented in state and federal criminal justice systems. Native Americans are incarcerated at a rate 38% higher than the national average and were overrepresented in the prison population in 19 states compared to any other race and ethnicity. The National Prisoner Statistics series of 2016 reported 22,744 Native Americans were incarcerated in state and federal facilities and represented 2.1 to 3.7% of the federal offender population during 2019 despite only accounting for 1.7% of the United States population. In states with higher Native American populations such as North Dakota, incarceration rates are up to 7 times that of their White counterparts. A study analyzing federal sentencing data found that Native Americans are sentenced more harshly than White, African American, and Hispanic offenders. In fact, further analysis showed that young Native American males receive the most punitive sentences, surpassing punishment imposed upon young, African American or Hispanic males.[^cite_note-Safety_and_Justice_Challenge-233]

The healthcare system also demonstrates disregard for Native American lives by creating additional barriers to accessing care in the state system, which places a higher burden on the [Indian Health Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Health_Service) that is already chronically underfunded and understaffed. Native Americans experience a higher rate of violent hate crime victimizations than any other race or ethnicity.[^cite_note-234] Overall, Native Americans continue to experience racism, oppression, discrimination, microaggressions, mockery, and misunderstandings of current day Nativeness. The tandem exoticization and devaluation of Native American lives contributes to the epidemic of disappearances and murders of Native Americans, paired with delayed or poor investigations of these occurrences.[^cite_note-Safety_and_Justice_Challenge-233]

## Asian Americans

Main articles: [History of Asian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Asian_Americans), [Racism against Asians § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism_against_Asians#United_States), and [Lynching of Asian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_of_Asian_Americans)

Further information: [Stereotypes of East Asians in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stereotypes_of_East_Asians_in_the_United_States) and [Asian American activism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_American_activism)

[Asian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans), including those of [East Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asian_people), [South Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_ethnic_groups), and [Southeast Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_of_Southeast_Asia) descent, have experienced racism since the first major groups of Chinese immigrants arrived in America. The [Naturalization Act of 1790](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalization_Act_of_1790) made Asians ineligible for citizenship.[^cite_note-Schultz-235] First-generation immigrants, the children of immigrants, and Asians who are adopted by non-Asian families are still impacted by discrimination.[^cite_note-Eguchi,_Shinsuke_2013-236]

During the [Industrial Revolution in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution_in_the_United_States), labor shortages in the mining and rail industries were prevalent. Chinese immigrant labor was often used to fill this gap, most notably with the construction of the [first transcontinental railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_transcontinental_railroad), leading to large-scale Chinese immigration.[^cite_note-Eguchi,_Shinsuke_2013-236] These Chinese immigrants were seen as taking the jobs of whites for lower wages, and the phrase [Yellow Peril](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_Peril), which predicted the demise of [Western civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture) as the result of Chinese immigration, gained popularity.[^cite_note-237]

### 19th century

See also: [History of Chinese Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Chinese_Americans), [Anti-Chinese sentiment § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Chinese_sentiment#United_States), [Anti-Chinese sentiment in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Chinese_sentiment_in_the_United_States), and [Anti-Chinese legislation in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Chinese_legislation_in_the_United_States)



A political cartoon from 1882 ridiculing the [Chinese Exclusion Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Exclusion_Act), showing a Chinese man, surrounded by benefits of Chinese immigration, being barred entry to the "Golden Gate of Liberty", while other groups, including [communists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism) and [hoodlums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoodlum), are allowed to enter. The caption reads sarcastically, "We must draw the line *somewhere*, you know."

In 1871, one of the largest [lynchings in American history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States) was committed against [Chinese immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Americans) in Los Angeles, California. It would go on to become known as the [Los Angeles Chinese massacre of 1871](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Chinese_massacre_of_1871). The 1879 Constitution of the State of California prohibited the employment of [Chinese people](372983.md) by state and local governments, as well as by businesses that were incorporated in California. Also, the 1879 constitution delegated power to local governments in California to enable them to remove Chinese people from the borders of their communities.[^cite_note-archives.cdn.sos.ca.gov-238][^cite_note-Hitler's_American_Model_2017_p._35-239] The federal [Chinese Exclusion Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Exclusion_Act) of 1882 banned the immigration of Chinese labourers for ten years. The [Geary Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geary_Act) of 1892 extended the Chinese Exclusion Act by requiring all Chinese citizens to carry their [resident permit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Permanent_Resident_Card) at all times or risk either deportation or a year of hard labor, and was upheld by the 1893 [Supreme Court](31737.md) case [Fong Yue Ting v. United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fong_Yue_Ting_v._United_States). Several mob attacks against Chinese people took place, including the [Rock Springs massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_Springs_massacre) of 1885 in [Wyoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wyoming) in which at least 28 Chinese miners were killed and 15 other Chinese miners were injured, and the [Hells Canyon massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hells_Canyon_massacre) of 1887 in [Oregon](26811621.md) in which 34 Chinese miners were killed.[^cite_note-\:0-240] In 1888, the [Scott Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Act_\(1888\)) prevented 20,000-30,000 Chinese abroad from returning to the United States and was later upheld in the 1889 Supreme Court case [Chae Chan Ping v. United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chae_Chan_Ping_v._United_States).



[Los Angeles Chinese massacre of 1871](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Chinese_massacre_of_1871)



Denver's anti-Chinese riot in 1880

Local discriminatory laws were also enacted to stifle Chinese business and job opportunities; for example, in the 1886 [Supreme Court](31737.md) case of *[Yick Wo v. Hopkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yick_Wo_v._Hopkins)*, a San Francisco city ordinance requiring permits for laundries (which were mostly Chinese-owned) was struck down, as it was evident the law solely targeted Chinese Americans. When the law was in effect, the city issued permits to virtually all non-Chinese permit applicants, while only granting one permit out of two hundred applications from Chinese laundry owners. When the Chinese laundries continued to operate, the city tried to fine the owners. In 1913, California, home to many Chinese immigrants, enacted an [Alien Land Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Alien_Land_Law_of_1913), which significantly restricted land ownership by Asian immigrants, and [extended it](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Alien_Land_Law_of_1920) in 1920, ultimately banning virtually all land ownership by Asians.[^cite_note-ReferenceB-241]

Japanese immigrants, who were unaffected by the Chinese Exclusion Act, began to enter the United States in large numbers in 1907, filling jobs that were once filled by Chinese workers. This influx also led to discrimination and President [Theodore Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt) restricted Japanese immigration. Theodore Roosevelt's [Executive Order 589](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Executive%20Order%20589) specifically prevented Japanese and Korean laborers, who possessed valid passports to go to Mexico, Canada, or Hawaii, from entering the continental United States. Later, Japanese immigration was closed when Japan entered into the [Gentlemen's Agreement of 1907](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentlemen's_Agreement_of_1907) to stop issuing passports to Japanese workers intending to move to the U.S.[^cite_note-242]

The immigration of people from all Asian countries was banned by the sweeping [Immigration Act of 1917](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_Act_of_1917), also known as the Asiatic Barred Zone Act, which also banned homosexuals, people with [intellectual disability](18567040.md), and people with an anarchist worldview.[^cite_note-Hitler's_American_Model_2017_p._35-239]

### Anti-Japanese sentiment and legislation

Main articles: [Anti-Japanese sentiment § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Japanese_sentiment#United_States), and [Anti-Japanese sentiment in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Japanese_sentiment_in_the_United_States)

See also: [Gentlemen's Agreement of 1907](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentlemen's_Agreement_of_1907), [California Alien Land Law of 1913](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Alien_Land_Law_of_1913), and [Internment of Japanese Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internment_of_Japanese_Americans)

### Anti-Filipino sentiment and legislation

In 1927, the four-day [Yakima Valley riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yakima_Valley_riots) in Washington state resulted in hundreds of Filipinos being forced to leave the valley under threat of death. In 1930, the [Watsonville riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watsonville_riots) in California involved a mob of 500 white men and youths causing five days of violent attacks on Filipino farm workers, and the death of one worker who was shot through the heart. In 1934, the [Tydings–McDuffie Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tydings%E2%80%93McDuffie_Act) allowed the [Philippines](23440.md), then an American colony, to become an independent country after ten years. The act established a quota of 50 Filipino immigrants to the United States per year. The [Filipino Repatriation Act of 1935](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_Repatriation_Act_of_1935) provided voluntary one-way passage for Filipinos in the United States to return to the Philippines. However, if they wanted to return to the United States, they would then be subject to the quota of 50 Filipino immigrants per year.

### World War II and postwar



Because of the war, the Japanese were seen as a threat to the home front.

During [World War II](32927.md), the [Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_China) was an ally of the United States, and the federal government praised the resistance of the Chinese against Japan during the [Second Sino-Japanese War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Sino-Japanese_War), in an attempt to reduce [anti-Chinese sentiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Chinese_sentiment_in_the_United_States). In 1943, the [Magnuson Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Exclusion_Repeal_Act) was passed by Congress, repealing the Chinese Exclusion Act and reopening Chinese immigration; at the time, the United States was actively fighting against the [Empire of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan), which was a member of the [Axis powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_powers). [Anti-Japanese racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Japanese_sentiment), which spiked after the [attack on Pearl Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack_on_Pearl_Harbor), was tacitly encouraged by the government, which used slurs such as "[Jap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jap)" in [propaganda posters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda_for_Japanese-American_internment). On February 19, 1942, President [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt) signed [Executive Order 9066](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_9066) which cleared the way for [internment of 120,000 Japanese Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internment_of_Japanese_Americans), citing possible security threats. American soldiers who fought in the [Pacific theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_War) frequently dehumanized their enemies, leading them to [mutilate Japanese war dead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_mutilation_of_Japanese_war_dead).[^cite_note-243] The racist nature of this dehumanization is revealed by the different ways in which corpses were treated in the [Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_War) and [European theaters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_theatre_of_World_War_II). Apparently, some soldiers mailed Japanese skulls home as souvenirs, but none of them mailed German or Italian skulls home.[^cite_note-244] This [prejudice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prejudice) continued to exist for some time after the end of the war, and anti-Asian racism also affected U.S. policy during the [Korean](16772.md) and [Vietnam Wars](32611.md), even though Asians fought on both sides during both of those wars as well as during World War II. Some historians have alleged that a climate of racism, with unofficial rules like the "mere gook rule",[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-246] allowed a pattern to exist in which [South Vietnamese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Vietnam) civilians were treated as if they were less than human and [war crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_war_crimes#Vietnam_War) were also common.[^cite_note-247] Despite poor treatment by the United States, thousands of [Japanese Americans joined the US military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_American_service_in_World_War_II) during World War II, in the segregated [442nd Infantry Regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/442nd_Infantry_Regiment_\(United_States\)) and [100th Infantry Battalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/100th_Infantry_Battalion_\(United_States\)). The 442nd suffered heavy losses during its fight against Nazi Germany while it was rescuing the [Lost Battalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lost_Battalion_\(Europe,_World_War_II\)), and in recognition of these combat casualties, it was nicknamed "The [Purple Heart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purple_Heart) Battalion."

On October 18, 1948, President [Harry S. Truman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_S._Truman) issued [Executive Order 10009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_executive_actions_by_Harry_S._Truman#1948) to revoke in part Executive Orders 589 from March 14, 1907, and Executive Order 1712 from February 24, 1913.[^cite_note-248]

### Anti-Indian sentiment



[Bhagat Singh Thind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhagat_Singh_Thind) was twice denied citizenship as he was not deemed white.

[^cite_note-249]

Prior to 1965, Indian immigration to the U.S. was small and isolated, with fewer than 50,000 Indian immigrants in the country. The [Bellingham riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellingham_riots) in [Bellingham, Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellingham,_Washington), on September 5, 1907, epitomized the low tolerance in the U.S. for Indians and [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindus). While anti-Asian racism was embedded in U.S. politics and culture in the early 20th century, Indians were also racialized, with U.S. officials casting them as "Hindu menaces" and pushing for Western imperial expansion abroad.[^cite_note-Seema-250] In the 1923 case, *[United States v. Bhagat Singh Thind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Bhagat_Singh_Thind)*, the Supreme Court ruled that high caste Hindus were not "white persons" and were therefore racially ineligible for naturalized citizenship.[^cite_note-Thind-251] The Court also argued that the racial difference between Indians and whites was so great that the "great body of our people" would reject assimilation with Indians.[^cite_note-Thind-251] It was after the [Luce–Celler Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luce%E2%80%93Celler_Act) of 1946 that a quota of 100 Indians per year could immigrate to the U.S. and become citizens.[^cite_note-252]

The [Immigration and Nationality Act of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_and_Nationality_Act_of_1965) facilitated entry to the U.S. for immigrants other than the traditional [Northern European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_European) and [Germanic groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_peoples), and as a result, it would significantly, and unintentionally, alter the demographic mix in the U.S.[^cite_note-Immigration_law-253] On the U.S. immigration laws prior to 1965, sociologist [Stephen Klineberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Klineberg) stated the law "declared that Northern Europeans are a superior subspecies of the white race."[^cite_note-Immigration_law-253] In 1990, Asian immigration was encouraged when nonimmigrant temporary working visas were given to help with the shortage of skilled labor within the United States.[^cite_note-Eguchi,_Shinsuke_2013-236]

### The Second Half of the 20th Century

Despite the many injustices done unto Asian-Americans in the 20th Century, one field of cultural interest that actually saw the opposite trend was [Traditional Martial Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_arts). This trend arguably began when American soldiers brought home, various Japanese and Okinawan martial arts that they had studied while living in Japan, in the years following the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War).[^cite_note-254][^cite_note-255][^cite_note-256] In other instances, local caucasian and black Americans who studied under Asian-American masters, helped popularized their styles and culture.[^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258] Such was the case of [Ed Parker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ed_Parker) with his art of [American Kenpo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Kenpo).[^cite_note-259] What else was instrumental in promoting this positive image of Asian-Americans, was existence of television shows such as [Kung Fu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kung_Fu_\(1972_TV_series\)). Due to these varying phenomenon Asian-Americans, and especially Chinese-Americans, became culturally cast as having a positive association with martial arts skills, which many amongst the American non-Asian public were then seeking to acquire.[^cite_note-260] The general cultural sentiment was expressed in art and popular as well, such as in the song [Kung Fu Fighting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kung_Fu_Fighting) and the band [Wu-Tang Clan](33135.md) - both of which were initiatives of African-Americans. All of this has contributed greatly to the rise of many notable Asian martial arts teachers in the United States, such as [Bruce Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_Lee), [Brandon Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brandon_Lee), [Dan Inosanto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Inosanto), [Fumio Demura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fumio_Demura), [Pat Morita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pat_Morita) (who portrayed a martial arts master in film but did not practice himself), [Jason Scott Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jason_Scott_Lee), [Mark Dacascos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Dacascos), and others.[^cite_note-261]

### 21st century

See also: [China Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Initiative)

Since the 20th century, Asians, particularly East Asians, have been [cast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stereotypes_of_East_Asians_in_the_United_States) as a "[model minority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Model_minority)". They are categorized as being more educated and successful, and they are also stereotyped as being intelligent and hard-working, but they are also stereotyped as being socially inept.[^cite_note-Lai,_Lei_2013-262] Asians may experience expectations of natural intelligence and excellence from whites as well as from members of other minority groups.[^cite_note-ReferenceB-241][^cite_note-263] This has led to discrimination in the workplace, as Asian Americans may face unreasonable expectations because of this stereotype. According to the *Journal of Organizational Behavior*, in 2000, out of 1,218 adult Asian Americans, 92 percent of those who experienced personal discrimination believed that the unfair treatment which they were subjected to was due to their ethnicity.[^cite_note-Lai,_Lei_2013-262] These stereotypes can also render the experience of the large number of Asians who are living in poverty in the United States invisible.

These stereotypes can also obstruct career paths; because Asians are seen as better skilled in engineering, computing, and mathematics, they are often encouraged to pursue technical careers. They are also discouraged from pursuing non-technical occupations as well as executive occupations which require more social interaction, since Asians are perceived as having poor social skills. In the 2000 study, forty percent of those surveyed who experienced discrimination believed that they had lost hiring or promotion opportunities. In 2007, the [Equal Employment Opportunity Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_Employment_Opportunity_Commission) reported that Asians make up 10 percent of professional jobs, while 3.7 percent of them held executive, senior level, or manager positions.[^cite_note-Lai,_Lei_2013-262]

Other forms of discrimination against Asian Americans include [racial profiling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_profiling) and [hate crimes](13547.md). The FBI noted that in 2015, 3.2 percent of all hate crimes involved anti-Asian bias.[^cite_note-264] In 2016, the [Seattle](11388236.md) Police Department reported that there was a 40 percent increase in race-based crimes against Asian Americans, both criminal and non-criminal.[^cite_note-265]

Research shows that discrimination has led to more use of informal mental health services by Asian Americans. Asian Americans who feel discriminated against also tend to smoke more.[^cite_note-266]

There have been widespread [incidents of xenophobia, racist bullying, and racist violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xenophobia_and_racism_related_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic#United_States) against Chinese Americans and other Asian Americans in response to the [COVID-19 pandemic](63136490.md).[^cite_note-NYT_Racism-267][^cite_note-268]

According to a poll done in 2022, 33 percent of Americans believe Asian Americans are "more loyal to their country of origin" than the US while 21 percent falsely believe Asian Americans are at least "partially responsible" for the COVID-19 pandemic.[^cite_note-\:3-269] Additionally, only 29 percent of Asian Americans believe they "completely agree" with the statement that they feel they belong and are accepted in the US, while 71 percent say they are discriminated in the US.[^cite_note-\:3-269]

According to a poll conducted in 2023, only 22 percent of Asian Americans completely agree that "Personally, I feel like I belong and am accepted in the U.S."[^cite_note-270] More than half of Asian Americans answered that they did not feel safe in public spaces.[^cite_note-271]

## European Americans

See also: [Religious discrimination in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_discrimination_in_the_United_States), [Anti-Catholicism § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Catholicism#United_States), [Anti-Catholicism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Catholicism_in_the_United_States), [Anti-French sentiment in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-French_sentiment_in_the_United_States), [Anti-German sentiment § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-German_sentiment#United_States), [Anti-Italianism § In the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Italianism#In_the_United_States), [Anti-Italianism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Italianism_in_the_United_States), [Anti-Polish sentiment § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Polish_sentiment#United_States), [Anti-Polish sentiment in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Polish_sentiment_in_the_United_States), [Anti-Russian sentiment § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Russian_sentiment#United_States), [Anti-Russian sentiment in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Russian_sentiment_in_the_United_States), [Anti-Slavic sentiment § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Slavic_sentiment#United_States), [Anti-Spanish sentiment § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Spanish_sentiment#United_States), [Nativism (politics) § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_\(politics\)#United_States), [Nativism in United States politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_in_United_States_politics), [Xenophobia § United States](34141.md#United_States), [Xenophobia in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xenophobia_in_the_United_States), [Anti-white racism § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-white_racism#United_States), and [History of the Catholic Church in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Catholic_Church_in_the_United_States)

Various [European American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Americans) immigrant groups have been subjected to discrimination on the basis of their [religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion) (see [Religious discrimination in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_discrimination_in_the_United_States) and [Anti-Catholicism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Catholicism_in_the_United_States)), immigrant status (which is known as "[Nativism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_\(politics\))") or ethnicity (country of origin).



[Philadelphia nativist riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia_nativist_riots)



*[New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Times)*, 1854 ad, reading "No Irish need apply."

In the 19th century, this was particularly true because of [anti-Irish prejudice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Irish_sentiment), which was based on [anti-Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Catholicism) sentiment, and prejudice against the Irish as an ethnicity. This was especially true for Irish Catholics who immigrated to the U.S. in the mid-19th century; the large number of Irish (both Catholics and Protestants) who settled in America in the 18th century had largely (but not entirely) escaped such discrimination and eventually blended into the white American population. During the 1830s in the U.S., riots over control of job sites broke out in rural areas among rival labor teams whose members were from different parts of Ireland, and riots also broke out between Irish and local American work teams which were competing for construction jobs.[^cite_note-272]

The Native American Party, commonly called the [Know Nothing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Know_Nothing) movement, was a political party that operated on a national basis during the mid-1850s, its membership was limited to [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_the_United_States) men who sought to limit the influence of Irish Catholics and other immigrants, thus reflecting [nativist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_in_United_States_politics) and [anti-Catholic sentiments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Catholicism_in_the_United_States). There was widespread anti-Irish job discrimination in the United States and "No Irish need apply" signs were common.[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274][^cite_note-LongIsland-275]



[Ku Klux Klan](16779.md) members march down Pennsylvania Avenue in Washington, D.C. in 1928. The second era Klan was a large nationwide movement with between four million and six million members.

The second era [Ku Klux Klan](16779.md) was a very large nationwide organization in the 1920s, consisting of between four million and six million members (15% of the nation's eligible population) that especially opposed Catholics.[^cite_note-276] The revival of the Klan was spurred by the release of the 1915 film *[The Birth of a Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Birth_of_a_Nation)*.[^cite_note-277] The second and third incarnations of the Ku Klux Klan made frequent references to America's "[Anglo-Saxon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Anglo-Saxon_Protestants) blood".[^cite_note-278] Anti-Catholic sentiment, which appeared in North America with the first Pilgrim and [Puritan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puritan) settlers in New England in the early 17th century, remained evident in the United States up to the presidential campaign of [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy), who went on to become the first Catholic U.S. president in 1961.[^cite_note-279]



Italian immigrants [Sacco and Vanzetti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacco_and_Vanzetti) were wrongfully executed in 1927; most historians agree that they were given an unfair trial heavily influenced by [anti-Italianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Italianism) and [anti-immigrant bias](6491626.md).

The 20th century saw discrimination against immigrants from [Southern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Europe) and Eastern Europe (notably [Italian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Americans) and [Polish Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Americans)), partially as a result of anti-Catholic sentiment (as well as discrimination against [Irish Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Americans)), partially as a result of [Nordicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordicism). The primary spokesman for Nordicism was the eugenicist [Madison Grant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madison_Grant). His 1916 book about Nordicism, *[The Passing of the Great Race, or the Racial Basis of European History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Passing_of_the_Great_Race)*, was highly influential among racial thinkers and government policy makers in the U.S.[^cite_note-280]

> Biological laws tell us that certain divergent people will not mix or blend. The Nordics propagate themselves successfully. With other races, the outcome shows deterioration on both sides.

— Future U.S. president [Calvin Coolidge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvin_Coolidge), 1921.[^cite_note-281]

Discrimination was also directed at people based on their physical appearances. Immigrants from [Southern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Europe) frequently had heavy tans and they also spoke foreign languages. These differences caused many [white Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Americans) to consider the darker immigrants [non-white](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Person_of_color). These differences were especially noticeable among immigrants who spoke [Romance languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romance_languages), which the majority of white Americans could not distinguish from [Spanish](26825.md); thus, immigrants who were from countries such as [Italy](14532.md) and [Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberia) and Hispanics who were from places like [Puerto Rico](23041.md) were considered people of color and discriminated against, a practice which has continued into the 21st century. Over time, as the children and descendants of the immigrants learned English with no accent, and as their tan skin color became lighter due to the fact that they were living in a nation which had a non-[Mediterranean climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_climate), these Europeans were more likely to be accepted as white. The largest mass [lynching in American history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States) was committed against Italians who lived in [New Orleans](53842.md) ( see [1891 New Orleans lynchings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1891_New_Orleans_lynchings)).[^cite_note-282][^cite_note-283][^cite_note-284]

An advocate of the U.S. immigration laws that favored Northern Europeans, the Klansman [Lothrop Stoddard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lothrop_Stoddard) primarily wrote about the alleged dangers which "[colored](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colored)" peoples posed to white civilization, with his most famous book *[The Rising Tide of Color Against White World-Supremacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Rising_Tide_of_Color_Against_White_World-Supremacy)* in 1920. Nordicism led to the reduction in Southern European, along with [Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavs) Eastern European and Russian immigrants in the [National Origins Formula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Origins_Formula) of the [Emergency Quota Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_Quota_Act) of 1921 and the [Immigration Act of 1924](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_Act_of_1924), whose goal was to maintain the status quo distribution of ethnicity by limiting immigration of non-Northern Europeans. According to the U.S. Department of State the purpose of the act was "to preserve the ideal of American homogeneity".[^cite_note-285] The racial term *[Untermensch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Untermensch)* originates from the title of Stoddard's 1922 book *The Revolt Against Civilization: The Menace of the Under-man*.[^cite_note-286] It was later adopted by the Nazis (and its chief racial theorist [Alfred Rosenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Rosenberg)) from that book's German version *Der Kulturumsturz: Die Drohung des Untermenschen* (1925).[^cite_note-287]

## Hispanic and Latino Americans

See also: [History of Hispanic and Latino Americans in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans_in_the_United_States), [History of Mexican Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Mexican_Americans), [Stereotypes of Hispanic and Latino Americans in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stereotypes_of_Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans_in_the_United_States), [Anti-Mexican sentiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Mexican_sentiment), and [2019 El Paso shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_El_Paso_shooting)



A rally is held for victims of [Hurricane Maria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Maria) in protest against the U.S. government's response to it and the [Political status of Puerto Rico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_status_of_Puerto_Rico).

Americans of [Latin American](18524.md) ancestry ([often categorized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_\(ethnic_categories\)) as "[Hispanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic)" or [Hispanic and Latino Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans)) come from a wide variety of racial and ethnic backgrounds. The nature of discrimination against them has often depended on how their physical appearance aligns with racial markers, particularly Black or Indigenous features.

After the [Mexican–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican%E2%80%93American_War) (1846–1848), the United States annexed much of the current [Southwestern region](179571.md) from Mexico. Mexicans who resided in that territory were subjected to discrimination. According to conservative estimates, 597 Mexicans were lynched between 1848 and 1928, corresponding to a per capita lynching rate second only to that suffered by the African American community.[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-autogenerated2-289]



Racist sign from the deep south ([National Civil Rights Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Civil_Rights_Museum))

Many public institutions, businesses, and homeowners associations officially excluded [Mexican Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Americans) as a matter of policy. School children of Mexican American descent were subjected to racial segregation in the public school system. In many counties, Mexican Americans were excluded from serving as jurors in court cases, especially in those that involved Mexican American defendants. In many areas across the Southwest, they lived in separate residential areas, due to laws and real estate company policies.[^cite_note-290][^cite_note-291][^cite_note-autogenerated5-292][^cite_note-293]



Hispanic protest against California immigration policy. *Todos somos ilegales – We are all Illegals*.

During the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression), the U.S. government sponsored a [Mexican Repatriation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Repatriation) program which was intended to encourage Mexican immigrants to voluntarily return to Mexico, however, many were forcibly removed against their will. At least 355,000 persons of Mexican ancestry went to Mexico during the 1930s, 40 to 60 percent of those individuals were U.S. citizens – overwhelmingly children. Voluntary repatriation was more common than formal deportation. The government formally deported at least 82,000 people to Mexico between 1929 and 1935.[^cite_note-gratton-294]

The [Zoot Suit Riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoot_Suit_Riots) were incidents of racial violence against Latinos in Los Angeles in 1943 which lasted several days.[^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296]

&#x20;During the 1960s, young Mexican Americans formed the [Chicano Civil Rights Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicano_Movement). U.S. president [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon) is recorded exhibiting prejudice toward Mexican Americans and African Americans:&#x20;

> I have the greatest affection for \[blacks], but I know they're not going to make it for 500 years. ... The Mexicans are a different cup of tea. They have a heritage. At the present time they steal, they're dishonest, but they do have some concept of family life. They don't live like a bunch of dogs, which the Negroes do live like.

— U.S. president [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon), 1971.[^cite_note-297]

## Middle Eastern and South Asian Americans

See also: [Anti-Middle Eastern sentiment § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Middle_Eastern_sentiment#United_States), [Anti-Iranian sentiment § United States](5356681.md#United_States), [Anti-Indian sentiment § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Indian_sentiment#United_States), and [Stereotypes of Arabs and Muslims in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stereotypes_of_Arabs_and_Muslims_in_the_United_States)



An [Assyrian church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACOE) after it was vandalized in Detroit (2007). Although they are not Arabs and are mostly [Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_Christianity), [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_people) often face a racist backlash in the US because of their Middle Eastern background.

[^cite_note-298]

People of [Middle Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Eastern_Americans) and [South Asian descent](11353572.md) historically occupied an ambiguous racial status in the United States. Middle Eastern and South Asian immigrants were among those who sued in the late 19th and early 20th century to determine whether they were "white" immigrants as required by naturalization law. By 1923, courts had vindicated a "common-knowledge" standard, concluding that "scientific evidence", including the notion of a "[Caucasian race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasian_race)" including Middle Easterners and many South Asians, was incoherent. Legal scholar John Tehranian argues that in reality this was a "performance-based" standard, relating to religious practices, education, intermarriage and a community's role in the United States.[^cite_note-299]

### Arab Americans

See also: [Arab Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Americans), [Arab immigration to the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_immigration_to_the_United_States), [Anti-Arab racism § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Arab_racism#United_States), [Anti-Palestinianism § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Palestinianism#United_States), [Islam in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_the_United_States), [Islamophobia in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamophobia_in_the_United_States), [Stereotypes of Arabs and Muslims in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stereotypes_of_Arabs_and_Muslims_in_the_United_States), and [United States foreign policy in the Middle East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_foreign_policy_in_the_Middle_East)

Racism against [Arab Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Americans)[^cite_note-Leonard-300] and racialized [Islamophobia](162173.md) against [American Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_the_United_States) have risen concomitantly with tensions between the [American government](195149.md) and the [Islamic world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world).[^cite_note-301] Since the [September 11, 2001, attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September_11,_2001,_attacks) in the United States, discrimination and [racialized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racialized) violence has markedly increased against Arab Americans and many other religious and cultural groups.[^cite_note-302] Scholars, including Sunaina Maira and Evelyn Alsultany, argue that in the post-September 11 climate, the markers of the [racialization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racialization) of Muslim Americans are cultural, political, and religious rather than [phenotypic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenotype).[^cite_note-303][^cite_note-304]

In addition to attacks against [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) Arabs based on their religion, numerous [Christian Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Arabs) (who until recently constituted a majority of Arab Americans) have also been attacked based on their appearances, as have [Assyrians and Chaldeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_Americans).[^cite_note-305] Non-Arab and non-Muslim Middle Eastern people, as well as [South Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asians) of different ethnic/religious backgrounds ([Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu), Muslims and [Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh)) have been stereotyped as "Arabs" and racialized in a similar manner. The case of [Balbir Singh Sodhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balbir_Singh_Sodhi), a Sikh who was murdered at a [Mesa, Arizona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesa,_Arizona) gas station by a [white supremacist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_supremacist) for "looking like an Arab terrorist" (because of the turban, a requirement of [Sikhism](27964.md)), as well as that of Hindus being attacked for "being Muslims" have achieved prominence and criticism following the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md).[^cite_note-306][^cite_note-307]

[Racial profiling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_profiling) has been a growing problem for Arab Americans since the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md). Particularly in airports, Arab Americans are often subject to heightened [security screening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airport_security), pre-boarding searches and interrogations, and sometimes, they are denied passage "based solely on the belief that ethnicity or national origin increases passengers' flight risk."[^cite_note-308]

On January 27, 2017, President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) signed [Executive Order 13769](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_13769), titled "Protecting the Nation from Foreign Terrorist Entry into the United States", otherwise known as the "Muslim Ban". Entry was suspended for persons coming from [Iran](14653.md), [Iraq](7515928.md), [Libya](17633.md), [Somalia](27358.md), [Sudan](27421.md), [Syria](7515849.md), and [Yemen](350939.md). More than 700 travelers were detained, and up to 60,000 visas were "provisionally revoked".

### Iranian Americans

See also: [Iranian Americans](2234641.md) and [Iran–United States relations](310477.md)



A man holding a sign that reads "deport all Iranians" and "get the hell out of my country" during a protest of the [Iran hostage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_hostage_crisis) in Washington, D.C. in 1979

The November 1979 [Iran hostage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_hostage_crisis) at the U.S. embassy in [Tehran](57654.md) precipitated a wave of [anti-Iranian sentiment in the United States](5356681.md#In_the_United_States), directed both against the [new Islamic regime](347268.md) and Iranian nationals and immigrants. Even though such sentiments gradually declined after the release of the hostages at the start of 1981, they sometimes flare up. In response, some Iranian immigrants to the U.S. have distanced themselves from their nationality and instead identify primarily on the basis of their ethnic or religious affiliations.[^cite_note-309]

Since the 1980s and especially since the 1990s, it has been argued, Hollywood's depiction of Iranians has gradually shown vilification of them.[^cite_note-310]

### Indian Americans

See also: [Indian Americans § Discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Americans#Discrimination), [Anti-Indian sentiment § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Indian_sentiment#United_States), and [India–United States relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93United_States_relations)

In the United States, [Indian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Americans) have sometimes been mistaken for [Arabs](2185.md) or [Muslims](19541.md), and thus, many of the same prejudices which have been experienced by Arab Americans have also been experienced by Indian Americans, regardless of their actual religious or ethnic background.

In the 1980s, a gang known as the [Dotbusters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dotbusters) specifically targeted Indian Americans in [Jersey City, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jersey_City,_New_Jersey) with violence and harassment.[^cite_note-311] Studies of [racial discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_discrimination), as well as [stereotyping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stereotyping) and [scapegoating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scapegoating) of Indian Americans have been conducted in recent years.[^cite_note-Dhillon-312] In particular, racial discrimination against Indian Americans in the workplace has been correlated with [Indophobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indophobia) due to the rise in outsourcing/[offshoring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offshoring), whereby Indian Americans are blamed for US companies offshoring [white-collar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-collar_worker) labor to India.[^cite_note-313][^cite_note-Yee-314] According to the offices of the Congressional Caucus on India, many Indian Americans are severely concerned of a backlash, though nothing serious has taken place yet.[^cite_note-Yee-314] Due to various socio-cultural reasons, implicit racial discrimination against Indian Americans largely go unreported by the Indian American community.[^cite_note-Dhillon-312]

Numerous cases of religious stereotyping of [American Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_the_United_States) (mainly of Indian origin) have also been documented.[^cite_note-315]

Since the [September 11, 2001, attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September_11,_2001,_attacks), there have been scattered incidents of Indian Americans becoming mistaken targets for [hate crimes](13547.md). In one example, a [Sikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhs), [Balbir Singh Sodhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balbir_Singh_Sodhi), was murdered at a [Phoenix](49121.md) gas station in a hate crime.[^cite_note-316] This happened after [September 11](5058690.md), and the murderer claimed that his [turban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turban) made him think that the victim was a Middle Eastern American.

## Jewish Americans

Main article: [Antisemitism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_the_United_States)

See also: [African American–Jewish relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_American%E2%80%93Jewish_relations), [Antisemitism by country § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_by_country#United_States), [History of antisemitism § United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_antisemitism#United_States), [History of antisemitism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_antisemitism_in_the_United_States), [History of the Jews in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_the_United_States), [Interminority racism in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interminority_racism_in_the_United_States), [Israel–United States relations](3449549.md), [List of antisemitic incidents in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_antisemitic_incidents_in_the_United_States), and [Lynching of American Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_of_American_Jews)



Protesters at the [Unite the Right rally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unite_the_Right_rally) carrying [Confederate flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_flag), [Gadsden flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gadsden_flag), and a [Nazi flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_flag)

[Antisemitism](1078.md) [has also played a role](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_antisemitism_in_the_United_States) in the [history of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_United_States). During the late 19th and early 20th centuries, hundreds of thousands of [Jews](25955086.md) escaped from the [pogroms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pogrom) which were occurring in Europe.[^cite_note-Takaki-JewishMigr-317]

Beginning in the 1910s, [Southern Jewish communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_the_Southern_United_States) were attacked by the Ku Klux Klan, which objected to Jewish immigration, and frequently used "[The Jewish Banker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_antisemitism)" caricature in its [propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda). In 1915, [Leo Frank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo_Frank) was lynched in Georgia while he was serving a life sentence after being convicted of [murder](20171.md).[^cite_note-lynching_openly_organized2-318] This event was a catalyst in the re-formation of the Ku Klux Klan.[^cite_note-time-319]

Universities, such as [Harvard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard), introduced [Jewish quotas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_quota) which effectively placed a limit on the number of Jews admitted to the university. According to the historian [David Oshinsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Oshinsky), "Most of the surrounding medical schools ([Cornell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornell_University), [Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_University), [Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Pennsylvania), and [Yale](34273.md)) had rigid quotas in place. In 1935 Yale accepted 76 applicants from a pool of 501. About 200 of those applicants were Jewish and only five got in." He notes that Dean Milton Winternitz's instructions were precise: "Never admit more than five Jews, and take no blacks at all."[^cite_note-320]

Events in [Nazi Germany](21212.md) attracted attention in the United States. Jewish lobbying for intervention in Europe drew opposition from the [isolationists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_non-interventionism), amongst whom was Father [Charles Coughlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Coughlin), a well known radio priest, who believed that the Jews were leading the United States into [the war](32927.md).[^cite_note-Coughlin-321] He preached weekly, overtly anti-Semitic sermons and, from 1936, he began the publication of a newspaper, *[Social Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Justice_\(newspaper\))*, in which he printed [anti-Semitic accusations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitic_canard) such as those which are contained in *[The Protocols of the Elders of Zion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Protocols_of_the_Elders_of_Zion)*.[^cite_note-322]

A number of Jewish organizations, Christian organizations, Muslim organizations, and academics consider the [Nation of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_of_Islam) [anti-Semitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_of_Islam_and_antisemitism). Specifically, they claim that the [Nation of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_of_Islam) has engaged in [revisionist and antisemitic interpretations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_denial) of [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust) and it exaggerates the [role of Jews in the Atlantic slave trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_views_on_slavery).[^cite_note-323] The [Anti-Defamation League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Defamation_League) (ADL) alleged that the NOI's Health Minister, Dr. Abdul Alim Muhammad, accused Jewish doctors of injecting [Blacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_people) with the [AIDS virus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS),[^cite_note-324] an allegation that Muhammad and *[The Washington Post](102226.md)* have refuted.[^cite_note-325]

Although Jews are usually considered white by mainstream American society, the relationship between Jews and the concept of whiteness remains complex, so some Jews prefer not to identify as white.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-326][^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328][^cite_note-Village_Voice_1993-329] Prominent activist and rabbi [Michael Lerner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Lerner_\(rabbi\)) argues, in a 1993 *[Village Voice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Village_Voice)* article, that "in America, to be 'white' means to be the beneficiary of the past 500 years of European exploration and exploitation of the rest of the world" and that "Jews can only be deemed white if there is massive amnesia on the part of non-Jews about the monumental history of anti-Semitism".[^cite_note-Village_Voice_1993-329]

On October 27, 2018, Robert D. Bowers opened fire in a synagogue in [Pittsburgh](25101.md) with an [AR-15-style rifle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AR-15_style_rifle) while he was shouting [anti-Semitic](1078.md) [racial slurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ethnic_slurs). [This attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburgh_synagogue_shooting) resulted in 11 dead and 6 wounded, leaving the assailant charged with 29 criminal counts, one of which was the obstruction of free exercise of religious beliefs.[^cite_note-330]

[Continuing antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_the_United_States_in_the_21st_century) has remained an issue in the United States and the 2011 *Survey of American Attitudes Toward Jews in America,* which was released by the [Anti-Defamation League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Defamation_League) (ADL), has found that the recent [world economic recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late-2000s_recession) increased the expression of some antisemitic viewpoints among Americans. Most of the people who were surveyed expressed [pro-Jewish sentiments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philo-Semitism), with 64% of them believing that Jewish people have contributed much to U.S. social culture. Yet the polling also found that 19% of Americans answered "probably true" to the [antisemitic trope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitic_trope) that "Jews have too much control/influence on [Wall Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wall_Street)" (see [Economic antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_antisemitism)) while 15% of Americans concurred with the related statement that Jews seem "more willing to use shady practices" in business than other people do. Reflecting on the lingering antisemitism of about one in five Americans, [Abraham H. Foxman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_H._Foxman), the ADL's national director, has argued, "It is disturbing that with all of the strides we have made in becoming a more tolerant society, anti-Semitic beliefs continue to hold a vice-grip on a small but not insubstantial segment of the American public."[^cite_note-adl-poll-331]

The JNS reported in 2023 that America was experiencing the worst eruption of antisemitism in its 250 year history.[^cite_note-332] Jews were the religious group that faced the most hate crime in the United States in 2022 according to an FBI report.[^cite_note-333] FBI director [Christopher A. Wray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_A._Wray) said that 60% of religious based hate crimes were on Jews.[^cite_note-334]

## Consequences

See also: [Racial inequality in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_inequality_in_the_United_States)

### Developmental

Using [The Schedule of Racist Events](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychometrics_of_racism) (SRE), an 18-item self-report inventory which assesses the frequency of racist discrimination, [Hope Landrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hope_Landrine) and Elizabeth A.
